JOTTINGS BY AN OLD

JOURNALIST.

THE SWEDENBORGIANS. A cat may look at a King, and a lucre -tyro in such matters way at least question a theological expert ; therefore when the Rev. W. 11. Claxton came from Bolton to Warrington to enlighten us respecting "The Writings" of the immortal Swede whose devoted dis- ciple he is, I ventured to submit to him a certain difficulty arising out of the subject. This the rev.l gentleman was good enough to deal with at length in I last week's" Examiner," and lam much obliged to him for the pains he has been at. The facts which he has taken the trouble to compile for my especial I instruction are of considerable interest and value, but, although relevant as far as they go, to toy mind they leave the main issue pretty much as it was. To engage in prolonged controversy upon such a point as is here raised wculd be to abuse the editor's hospitality ; but I crave one more word before I pass to " the order of the day." Whether or not the work in which Sweden- borg refers to the state of the Psalmist David and the Apostle Paul in the life beyond was intended for publication seems to me be-ide the question. As to the former, I do not care at all if the spirit supposed to have been that of David was an impersonation : so I may disregard the quotation from the work cf the Rev. Ge rge Bush. What I am really concerned about is that Swedenborg should have represented St. Paul as con- sorting with evil spirits; and that Mr. Clayton and other New Church IT inisters should be so loth to let go the diary which contains these extraordinary entries. I may not enlarge upon or add to what I have written, feeble and inadequate as it may be deemed. l'or myself, however, to he quite candid : I have no use for a religious system which admits of even the bare possibility of uncertainty in relation to the eternal destiny of the great apostle of the Gentiles. Surely the Saviour who, with the assurance that he should that day be with Him in Paradise, cheered the penitent one of two malefactors between whom be was crucified, would not everlastingly condemn his faithful servant Paul! ESPERANTO.

ir week ago I prcmised to dip into a text book c the new international language, and to give the result in this column. My daughter and I together took Helen Fryer's "Teacher," and " swotted " at it for three or four evenings. Then, laying aside the book and making up my mind not to refer to that or any other authority, I wrote to the younger student— Kara Esperantisto,- Ni voles at vi coolie felica return° de Is tago. Kiln atno. Gepatrt j." The signa- ture is a joint one, my wife being associated with the message. I have no doubt that, even if the compositor be equal to the occasion and the proof-reader survive the shock, Warrington Esperantists will discover many faults in my elementary effott. For that I am prepared, and care not in the least for one could not fall into gentler bands. Here, however, is my point; not only was my shoit and simple message easily read and understocd by the one to whom it was addressed ; it wcu'd have been equally comprehensible by one of- any other nation or tongue who had devoted a few hours to examination of the " Novo lingvo." H that be so—and deny it who can—is it not very significant? THAT UMBRELLA. "Only one - one umbrella have I got; only one— just enough; don't want a lot. I visited Friend Jones and saw two splendid g,amps behind his door. When I had gone he said "Oh, I.or, there's only one !" So, on after that fashion (I quote from memory) ran a song which had a certain vogue perhaps a score years ago Jokes innumerable have been made upon the some subject befoie and since. Where would the profes- sional humorist be but for his mother-in-law, his cheese, the lodger, and, above all, his umbrella? According to that easy moralist, nobody ever thinks of returning a borrowed umbrella, and none have any scruples about stealing one. To be sure, it ought not to be described as theft—" convey, the wise it call." Yet here we have the manager of the Royal Court Theatre, through the medium of an advertisement, notifying the unauthorised dis- appearance of a " brolly" from that popular place of entertainment, and actually requesting the holder to send it back. " Kindly return same" quotha ! Surely this is the " simple faith " that is better—ever so much better—than "Norman blood." For myself, after all, I should not be surprised to hear that the umbrella which went astray on the Thursday night of the " Whip" performance weie once more revising in the bosom of its family, so to express it; for, look you, some time in the last century, I spent an evening at a diarama (a once attractive form of entertainment which the animated pictures have elbowed into oblivion), left any umbrella behind me. and, missing it four days afterwards, recovered it and bore it thence in tiiumph. THE LATE WILSON BARRETT. In his personal impressions of " Richard le Galliene," Alderman Bennett incidentally referred to " the great tragedian WilFolll Barrett." The latter was strongly attached to Halifax, in the West Riding, his mother being buried there. Before he became an actor-manager—while, indeed, be was comparatively unknown—l often saw him and his wife tad his brother perform in the old Theatre Royal there. On a memorable Saturday night, some 40 years ago, I saw Wilson Barrett in " The Bells" and ' The Corsican Brothers," and I did not sleep very well after it. As for his gifted wife, who died at the height of her powers, Miss Heath played the principal part in "East Lynne " so realistically that it generally made her ill ; and her patrons —well, it was awful, or so I found it. Once on such an occasion I acted as escort to a young lady who had read the book and very much wished to see the play. In the midst of the agony, my companion turned to me and sobbed" Is-is-isn't it ni-i-ce ? " What could I say ? I replied " Yes, er that is, you know why-er for those who like this sort of thing—well, of course it's the sort of thing they like." Perhaps it was excusable in the peculiar circumstances ; anyhow, I was then a young man. I alluded to the great tragedian's brother. Do any of my older readers remember George Et.ntrett ? I have seen and admired him as clown in pantomine—old style, of course. He was a clever comedian, and, although he never really achieved fame, he would undoubtedly have made his mark in these days. LAZY HUSBAND'S DESERTS.

At the period of which I have just written, such condnct as was revealed at Lymm Petty Sessions last week, and :3 rather frequently the subject of investi- gation in our local police courts, would not have been long tolerated by the habitual offender's own mates. At that time, the bellman was an institution, and he would be sent round publicly to expose and decry an erring husband. More than that, when a married man so far lost his self-respect and forgot what was due to his help-meet as to set off drinking and then beat his wife, his fellows would band themselves b•gether, fasten him on a handcart, and, with misdeeds writ large upon a board, run him round the main thoroughfares amid the jeers and taunts of a hcstile crowd. That is how a former generation sought to" make the punishment fit the came "—and in some cases, like the ancient stooks, it .ras a more effectual way than the regular legal process.

THE HOLY LANI,.

A well known local vicar has lately toured Egypt ' and the Holy land. and I understand and I can well , believe that he had a good and enviable time. Not so a humble friend of mine who was one of a band of English and American pilgrims on a " personally conducted " visit to Palestine a few years ago. lam ' not sure that he has even yet unite recovered from the effecta of the diet especially; and he seems to have been scared well nigh to death into the bargain. First a working collier in the neighbourhood of Ashton, and DOW kgeneral dealer in another part of Lancashire, and all along an enthusiastic Sunday school man, he carefully boarded his spare cash until the tour upon which his heart was set became possible. And he enjoyed it exceedingly—in a sense. Certainly he did not at all like the way in which bia native charioteer handled the ribbons; that individual's driving would appear to be still too much like unto that ofJelm, the non of Nimbi, of old, for he regarded neither the vehicle ef which he had charge nor the passengers it carried, and, the roads being rough, his recklessness was disquieting. Moreover, the bread of the country was so sour that it affected the Lancashire man's health, and be well nigh broke down. It is co be borne in mind that this was necessarily a cheap excursion—cheap and nasty. Ihe Rev. W. Bracecamp travelled under different conditions, and has quite a different story to tell. 1 THE PSALM OF LIFE. , Presiding at the distribution of prizes and certifi- cates at Holy Trinity branch technical school, Mrs. I C. Jackson Holmes took occasion to commend Long- fellow's" Psalm of Life." I was somehow reminded I of a parody which I met with more than 30 years t ago. I recollect that one verse went something like . this :

" Lives of grocery men remind its They may make their starch half lime, And with sugar wholly blind us, Putting sand in all the time !" EDUCATION. At a similar function at the Gymnasium, Mr. Holman Kingdom made us.e of Aristotle's definition of education. Education has also been defined as the training of the human faculties so that they may correspond with the law of progress by which man- kind is advancing to its highest enjoyment of life. I take it that few of those taking part in the interesting local gatherings now referred to will object to this ; but what will they say of the view taken by Mr. A. C. Benson, a while ago and expressed in the" Academy" than:—" We have somehow got into our heads that everyone has an inalienable right to be educated, but we are still in the dark as to the objects of education; instead cf thinking it is a process which ought to end in making men and women more simpleonore &intent, more happy, we had a vague idea that it will enable us to retain our commercial superiority and to keep ahead of America and Germany." No doubt a good deal might be said on both sides. Emphatically and pre-eminently it is a bread.and-butter question at the same time it is, or ought to be, something higher I and nobler than that.

DOMESTICS AND THE INSURANCE BILL.

Madam, do you keep a servant? If so, you probably pay her during illness, at any rate during stort ill- nesses, and you pay for her doctor. But what about a permanent breakdown, or long continued illness. The very rich often pension broken-down servants, possibly you cannot afford to ; but if your servant does break down, either you must provide for her, or you will always feel uncomfortable about it. This is where the Insurance Bill comes in. For 2d. a week it Lkes the respensibility off you and provides for the maid, if necessary for life; it also provides free doctor. - . .. .

Look through the housekeeping accounts and see what doctor's visits to the maid have cost you; unless you are most fortunate you will find that the doctor has cost you more than 10s. 10J. a year, or 2.1 d a week. So that you get your maid insured against consumption long. sickness or permanent breakdown without its costing you one penny piece. If you like to pay another half-penny a week, the Insurance will be , extended to cover sick pay in short illnesses, but that Is for you to decide. This Insurancs Bill will Iws an actual saving to you : hut what about your servant ? She has provision for breakdown guaranteed, and not dependent only on the good will or means of her employer : sickpay, disablement, and free doctor and hospital for life ; and she pays only 2d. a week for that. If she pays 3d. she gets in return double pay- ment when sick—whatever wages you give her and is. 6d, benefit as well, and she remains Insured when she leaves your service to get married or retire. A servant retiring at the age of 57, can remain insured for life without paying any further contribution. You ran find in every Workhouse, old servants for whom their employers could not or would not provide, It would be shameful, when the Bill is taking every other regular worker out of the reach of the Poor Law, to leave domestic servants uninsured in peril of work- house degradation for themselves and liable at any moment to bec( me a burden on the rates. Both mistresses and servants should support the Bill,

SCHOOL ARCHITECT.

EDUCATION COMMITTEE DECLINES TO CREATE NEW OFFICE.

"No Sweating or Jerry-Building."

The most important debate at the meeting of the Warrington Education Committee waged to end the proposal of the Sites and Buildings Committee to appoint 'school architect to design and superintend the erection of new Council schools instead of throwing each scheme open to competition. The meeting was presided over by Councillor li Roberts and there weir also present :—lttir A J Jolley (deputy-chairman), the Mayor (Councillor Dr. Joseph), Aldermen J Smethurst and A Bennett, Couocillors T Births, C Jackson Holmes, .1 Pemberton, W J Wilson, J Dickenson, R Hones/ill, W H J Jenkins, 'l' 9 easel, Revs Canon Stevenson, J Davies and N Wilson. Mrs C Jackson Holmes, Messrs C S Burgess, C E l'arker, R T Fair- dough, J Chattier', E Plington, H Riverton, J Upson, a Walker, C It linsworth, F II Roberts, .1 Moore Murray (director of education), R B Roberts (assiatant director), and J Fairhurst (borough treasurer). The Sites end Buildings Committee recommended that an advertisement be issued for an architectural assistant to the surveyor to the committee, to be appointed to design (bet not prepare the quantities) and act as clerk of works during the erection of the four contemplated new schools, to be directly respons- ible to the committee, to devote his whole time to their servicee. and to be accommodated ie the Surveyor's department ; the commencing salary to heat the rate of .C2OO per annum, all maple:tents to be between the erre. of 'l5 sod 50 years. The four proposal new schools should be completed within the next six yeareand it was understood that the architect's services would terminete on the completion of the work. If the new schools were se'd for on the same lines as the Boamont and Bolton schools, the architect's fees for desigus and quantities would arnount to £3,909 land the clerk of work's fees to £B9l. If the recommend. ation of the Committee core approved and a loan sanctioned, the annual repayment of sinking fund and interest is rcepect of the new at:hoots would be £ll2 as compered with £267 Han architect in private praotioe and a clerk of works were employed, and the total amount repaid in thirty years would be £3,960 as against £e,olo—a difference of £1,650. Assuming for the moment that the Local Government Board would not sanction the payment of the architect by means of a loan and that consequently the money would be psid out of revenue, the cost to the Committee for six sears , would be about the same as if the Committee proceeded I on the old method, but there would be as aving during , the remaining 24 years of £267 per annum, ova total of £6,408. The Committee wished to mention that there were other considerations besides the financial ones, and they would refer the members of the Education Committee to that part of the report of the Board of Education on the coat of school buildings which dealt with the results of the direct employment of architects by Local Authorities. IA letter signed by thy architects practising In War- rington had been addressed to the Education Com- mittee. The signatories laid arose on the accumulated I experience of many pheses of building work which the architect in geoeral could not uphold, either from the point of slew of experiecoe or economy in working ; and they ventured to tuggest to the Committee that they should, as in the cases of the Beamont and Bolton Council Schools, submit their schemes to local competition, on the lines laid down by the Royal kali. tut* of British Architects. Mr. R. T. FAIR. LOUGH, in moving the adoption of the minutes, said the committee had for some time been considering the best way in which they could carry on the work of school building. At one new school they had an architect and a clerk of works. Twelve months after the erection of the school they found it bad not been built according to speciftcation. The builder, however, was only to be held responsible for anything that occurred within six months of the completion of the contracts ; the Town Clera advised that they could not get at the architect, and it was no use going to the clerk of worke. The committee naturaily felt very much annoyed about it. They resolved upon another scheme to make the architects responsible, tut the Board of Education now recom- mended the direct employment of architects by local authorities. Hitherto the Education Committee had never psid anything for the services of the Borough Surveyor and his staff. That ought not to be. They ought to bear their fair share. If they appointed an architect, they thought ha would be able to take up the work that was now being paid for in some other department. Councillor HD:SHALL seconded the adoption of the minutes. . .

--- Alderman BENNETT moved an amendment that the report of the sub-committee be not adopted. He con- tended that the figures which had been submitted were very illusory. Even if they got an architeat on that cheap-Jack principle which seemed to commend itself to some of the members, the cost would inevitably be more than the committee anticipated, because they appeared to have lost sight of other necessary expenses that would arise. He hoped the committee would not be led away merely by the fact that there appeared, on the face of it, to be a considerable saving. There were some gentlemen who would vote for anything on God's earth if it only seemed to show an apparent saving—whether it was a wise policy or otherwise. The real consideration, however, ought to be: Were they getting value for mosey? They might save a few hundreds, or even thousands, and still be worse off in the long run. He was inclined to think that would be the probable result of the proposed new method. The figures looked very impressive but he did not think they would hold water and, if all the pros and cons were put together in a fair and reasonable way, they would find that the economy was more apparent than real. There were, however, other points to be borne in mind. First there was the feeling they ought to enrry into municipal life of doing something like justice to people who paid rates in the towa and had to get their living in it. They ought to give some con- sideration to their local architects. They had not much chance nowadays of building anything very noble and many of them must feel keenly within their ambitious bosoms &desire to do something worthy of their high professson and of their abilities. If they did not give them the opportunity of competing for the schools the authorities would probably !gradually take other things from their purview, and they would have little else to which to devote their httention hot the erection of eternal rows of cottages, with hideous excresences and backyards, and a little red and yellow brick as the only attempt at colour and imagination. (Laughter.) He did not think that in the long run the committee would get the buildings most suitable for the purpose they tied in view if they adopted the proposed cheese-paring and parsimonious method. The black-legging idea— paying less than the market price—was considered highly improper in the labour world, and he did not think the Education Committee ought to have a lower ideal than the leaders of the great trade unions. They ought to be prepared to pay their architects proper fees for proper work, lie did not want futile extravagance, but dignity and decency, and the best obtainable at a reasonable cost. If it bad been pro- posed to establish a new municipal department and a borough architect to deal with tho whole of the Corporation's architectural work at a salary commen- surate with its importance, providing proper appliances and staff, there would have been something to say for it. He was not sure whether, in spite of the local architects, he would not be in favour of that, for they would all have a chance of competing. He did not think any local architects would think of competing for the position of school architect at the salary offered If the committee went outside they might, by a stroke of good luck, get a genius, but the chances were that they would get mediocrity and the schools would suffer As a mere matter of pounds, shillings and penoe, they would not be much the gainers, and from the point of view of public utility and artistic taste they would be much the losers. LOCAL PARLIAMENT.

LIBERALS INTRODUCE MANHOOD SUFFRAGE.

Considerable interest was taken in the debate on the Electoral Reform Bill, introduced by the Prime Minister (Nlr. E. Wynne Jones) at the weekly meeting of the Warrington Parliamentary Debating Society on Tuesday evening. The measure, which was published in last week's "Examiner," proposes to establish manhood suffrage, to abolish plural voting, to fix one day on which all elections shall be hold, to close all licensed houses on general election days, to arrange a sysom of transferable voting, and to provide for a re-dis- tribution of seats throughout the United Kingdom. In the event of there being more than two candidates for one Parliamentary seat each elector will be given the power of indicating to which candidate he desires his vote to be transferred if the candidate of his first choice is third or lower on the poll, provided no candidate Ina a majority of the votes cast. Electors will be compelled to walk to the polls. The Bill prohibits canvassing, providi 8 for the State piyment of returning officer's expenses, and limits a candidate's personal expenses to £n)00, the amount to be computed from a date at least six months prior to the date of the election. Any candidate who spends either by himself or by any organisation working on his behalf above £5OO is to he unseated. The PRIME MINISTER said the measure would involve the gr.test exteneion of the franchise since 1530. It would make citizenship the one qualification for the vote and would simplify the system of regietration. Recent events had made a reform of the electoral system absolutely necessary. They had seen thousands of peop'e disfranchised through some subtle legal point and that was sufficient plea of urgency for the measure. Two amendments—one of them very far-reaching—had teen put in and they would be left an open question for the House. Every man had a right to share in the Government of the nation. If he did not pay rates and tales, he contributed to the national wealth by his labour. The granting of manhood suffrage world knit together the various sections of the community rather than divide them. With the passing of the Bill legislation would become wider, more just and more beneficent. The increase of the electorate under the Bill as at present drafted would be 3,000,000. He maintained that the young men they proposed to enfranchise were among the most intelligent and attractive members of the community. The conferring of the rights of citizenship would produce an increased sense of responsibility. In future the vote would be given to the individual and not to the property. The Premier touched upon the glaring anomalies of the present electoral systeru,point- ing out that thousands of people were dishaachised every year because they removed from one constituency to another. Once a man was on the register, he ought to be able to take his vote with him, wherever he went. (Hear, hear.) Plural voting had to go. (Applause.) It was absolutely inconsistent with representative government that one man should he entitled to greater electoral power than another. Be challenged the Opposi- tion to defend the plural voter, who, be said, was so dear to their hearts. The abolition of the plural vote would not disfranchise a single elector, but it would square matters in the right way. With regard to transferable voting, they wanted the members of the House of Commons to ben reflection of their constituencies—a mirror of the voice of the people. They wanted to avoid such cases as the Oldham election—(Opposition appbuse)—where the Conservative member represented only 401 per cent. of the electorate. (A voice : " He's the member.") More's the pity. Fifty-nine and a half percent. of the electors of Oldham had declared against the policy for which the member stood. If there had been a transferable vote, the majority of the Labour votes would have (roue to the Liberal candidate. (Opposition assent.) Defend- ing the closing of public houses on election day, the Premier said they wanted the electorate to give a sober vote. (Opposition laughter,) The great mass of the drink interest was allied to one party in the State. With regard to the proposed redistribution of seats, they wanted to correct not only the over-representation of Ireland, but the under-representation Of Liberalism in the English counties. (Opposition laughter.) The member for the Rumford division represented as many voters as fourteen Tory members in the present House of Commons. The democracy, the Premier concluded, was coming into its own, and the more power they gave to the people, the better it would be for the country ea a whole. (Applause.) Mr. N. Coot, jun. (Attorney General) formally seconded the adoption of the Bill. Mr. W. H. STANSFIETD (Lender of the Opposition) moved the usual amendment that the Bill be read six months hence. He said that, like many ether measures presented by the Liberal Government, the Bill had been conceived in a burry and was most illogical and mitchievons. In the first place it placed half the nation on a lower level than aliens, convicts or lunatics. While such persons were not, according to the Bill, to be deprived of the franchise, the Government denied the vote th women. Ile contended that by presenting such a Bill the Government had thrown an insult in ihs face of every Woman in the country. Every argument which had been advanced for muss exercise of the rights of citizenship applied with equal force to women. If the Goverement had not wanted to go I all the way, they might have stipulated that the vote I should be conferred upon "all married male persons." I There would have been some reason in that, as they ' would have got the influence of the women in that way. (Laughter). In order to render it stall work- able, the Bill would reqnire as much chopping and changing as the Insurance Bill, and then it would be worth nothiig. (Laughter). The provision that the returning officer's expenses should be borne by the State gave an opening to any demagogue, for any frivolous reason, to thrust himself forwerd ss • Parliamentary candidate. (A voice " Why should be not ? NOTES BY " OVALIST."

Nilled again! The " Wirepullers" will not look back With any satisfaction on the work of the past fortnight, and there is no manner of doubt that their supporters are 'disgruntled." It was bad enough to go down at home before Wigan, but to take a full team to Bull and lose 11-0 was worse.

Warrington are usually equal to a good performance at Hull—they have won there with a team half com- posed of reserves -and on the form of Hull during the reason Warrington's prospects were distinctly bright. So alluring were they, in fact, that about 400 ardent enthusiasts patronised the special train and gave the team an ardent welcome at the Boulevard. Instead of being inspired correspondingly, the Warrington men gave about their most ragged exhibition and allowed a weak Hull team to outpoint them with ease. Hull people rejoiced at the revival of the " Airly Birds," but their excellence was largely due to Warrington's mediocrity.

On Saturday it was the fault of the backs. The forwards played a grand game and gave the men behind enough chances to have scored 20 points. But they were bungled in a way that would have made Jack Fish weep- or any things unfit for publication. brooks and Creevey had an tff-day and some of the passes they heaved out to the centres were in danger of going over the stand. Others were intercepted, and altogether it was a heart-breaking exhibition. Luckily the defence was pretty good, and Tilley did wonders at times when all hope had fled. But some- thing will have to be done with the attack. Waywell seems to be the only man with a n, se for the line and like Woodburn he gets few chances. A strong fist centre will be worth a million bye and bye !

To-day Warrington have a chance of finding their form at the expense of Ebbw Vale, who usually leave points behind on their Northern visits. The Com- mittee have done a re-shuftle in the hope of turning p an are—though the " joker " is more likely ! up has been dropped once more, and Brookes put in the centre. This should improve the three- quarter line, but what about the halves ? Lloyd as partner to Creevey does not unfold a dazzling pros- pect, especially in view of the shocking display of Lloyd for the "A " team last Saturday. And neither ,can tackle. Well, it may do against Ebbw Vale, but the situation is obviously getting desperate.

The selection is as follows : Tilley, full hack ; Woodburn, Brooks, Bradshaw, and Pi'aywell, three- quarters ; Lloyd and Creevey, half backs ; Thomas, Johnston. Sbugars. Arnold, Chester; and Bradshaw, forwards.

FEEDING OF SCHOOL CHILDREN.

SUPPLY OF FREE MEALS RESUMED.

Standard of Necessity "Ludicrously Low."

The provision of meals for necessitous school children was again under consideration at tha monthly meeting of the Warrington Education Committee on Monday evening. It was reported that at a meeting of the School Attendance Sub-committee held or November 2nd, a petition from the Warrington Trades and Labour Council upon the subject of the provision of free meals for necessitous school children WAS read. Inquiries having been made at the schools. it was resolved, at n meeting of the committee on Ndvember 20th That free dinners he provided on school days to those children who have been found to come within the committee's scheme, and that they be supplied at a centre at the Education Office, commencing on Wednes- day, the 22nd November, IbII ; that Mrs. Mary Wilson be appointed cook, at a weekly wage of 205., her engage- ment to commence on the 21st November, 1911, and to be determinable by one week's notice on either si le ; that the Director be empowered to arrange for the psyrcent, where necessary and possible, of the tram fares of the children to enable them to attend at the centre at the Education office; that in cures where the income of the family has been found to be above the committee's scale, a letter of warning be sent to the parentv,and that the head teachers be asked to report later on as to whether there has been any improvement in the condition of the children that, in raves where the parents are in receipt of poor law relief, the Board of Guardians be informed of the fact 0.111 reported by the head teachers with a view to the Guardians cuing their influence with the parents to secure the proper application by the parents of the relief granted to them by the Guardians ; that, in cases where the Director is satisfied that the parents are in a position to do no, but are neglecting to properly feed their children, the psrents be summoned to appear before this committee for the purpose of their being warned of the possibility of legal proceedings being instituted against them under the Children fAct, 1918 ; and i) that, in cases where there is no one of sufficient age at home to prepare the meals for the children, the parents be visited fcr the purpose of their being influenced, if possible, to make improved arrangements. . .

Mr. LINSTOv, in moving de adoption of the minutes, said there was a great deal to be said for the Trades and Labour Council's proposal, for many would admit that where the family income was less than 21.9 d. per head, in face of the increased price of foodstuffs it was difficult for the children to be sufficiently (alto enable them to obtain proper results from the education they received. The committee ought not to throw the matter on one side es unfit for notice, simply because it came from a body that many people did not agree with. Twenty-nine children, from twelve familien, required feeding under the committee's scale of 2s. 6d. per head There were els° the cases of children who were not being properly fed but whose parents are getting considerably over ' the amount fixed by the committee. Those children ought not to be allowed to softer, but when they had been fed by the committee the parents should be pro- ceeded against for neglecting their duties. There were 353 children from 125 families In that category. The committee ought to be prepared to adopt the scheme no that they could at once take steps to see that no child attending school in Warrington was under-fed. The Rev. FATHER WILSON seconded. . .

if7-reilVtOTlr. Upson, the DIRECTOR said the scheme was exactly similar to the one that was in force two years ago. The CHAIRMAN (Councillor H. Roberts) said there was nothing conk ntioua in the scheme. It seas the duty of the committee to see that no child went to school without being properly fed. Out of the number reported upon there were 29 cases where the parents had an income below the Beale, and consequently the responsibility for feeling the children directly fell upon the committee. In the other cases the income of the parents was above the scale—in some cues con- siderably above—and, therefore, it became the duty of the committee to call the attention of the parents to the fact that their children bad been reported es being iustifficiently fed and to inform them that the committee would take the responsibility of feeding them, and afterwards if the parents did not the committee could recover the money and possibly take steps of a punitive cLaracter. Councillor PEMBERTON, after contending that the question had been brought forward too hurriedly, said be had always held that the committee's scale was too low. He could not see now parents could feed their children properly on such an amount. Tbe Rev. J. DAN lES said some of the children might receive proper nutriment at home and not he able to digest it. They would then have the appearance of being under fed. The committee should not be too hasty in charging the parents with neglect but, in addition to melting inquiries, should aleo take medical advice. _

--- Councillor DIChiVSON : Inquiries are made by our officers, sport from the teachers. Mr. UPSON eeled whether the committee intended to take into consideration the revision of their standard of necessity, which seemed ludicrously low. A family of six might be receiving lees than £1 a week and yet be debarred from relief. The increasing prices of food made it difficult for people with very smakwages to ensure that their children had sufficient food.

The eItAIRILIAN said the question of the scale did not come ill, for disenesion that night, bet he would not be sorry if Mr. Upson raised it at another meeting, with a view to its revision.

Mr. UPSON said there was considerable difficulty in finding out which cases were really necessitous. The children were under observation constantly but there were casea the scheme did not quite meet. Not only in saint, r but in summer a father would fall out of wort., or worked irregularly, and the family suffered. The committee scheme did not meet those cases, nor did the Guardians consider them, and the result was that there was a certain amount or distress very frequently in the schools. If they could have some modification of the scheme whereby the children could be fed. perhaps at some restaurant in Canes of great necessity, it would be a good thing. One of his teachers was threatened with Oleical violence the other day because she asked the whole of the class whether they had had any breakfast, and the person who offered the violence was probably the person who probably did not provide sufficient food for her children. The parents were, however, beginning to see that what the committee bed in view was for the good of thechildrea and they appreciated it. Alderman BENNiTT agreed that if the scale did not adequately meet the necessities of the case it ought to be revised. There could only he one opinion as to the general idea, underlying the scheme, end that was that if children were sent to school, from whatever cause, without proper physical sustenance, it was the duty of the committee to see that it was remedied. (Hear, hear). What woo the use of trying to cram their minds if their bodies were neglected ? He could not regard this country as a civilized nation as long as it was possible for any child to be sent to school starving. He had great sympathy with the parents. It was marvellous how many of them managed to feed their children on the wages they received. We were existing in a barbarous state of things which we called civiliza- tion but which was nothing of the kind. Until they got a minimum wage, which was available for any man who was willing to work, we would never solve these problems satisfactorily. In the meantime the author'. I ties ought to administer palliative measures ins broad and generous spirit. I Mr. KINt.DON said that, while he sympathised moat heartily with what had been said by the last two I speakers, it seemed to him that what the resolution asked was to decide that night whether they were going to feed the necessitous children or not. The question of the scale did not arise, They heard a groat dtal adout the responsibility of the parent, and this was a legitimate attempt to enforce that responsibility. From that point of view he should most heartily support the motion. (Hear, hear,) The Dance TOR stated that under the previous scheme there were only two awes In which the parents were considered to be in a position to keep the children. They were prosecuted, a ith the result that in nue case I the meals had to be paid for, bat in the other the man ' proved insofficient means. In other cases the warning I to parents who were neglecting their duties had the ' desired effect. What the committee proposed to do in the case of the 20 children who were entitled to free ' meals was very little to help to keep them in proper condition. They were only feeding them on school days and leaving them to starve on Stturday and Sunday. They were bound to have Christmas holidays, which meant another fortaight's starvation, and at Eater there would be another week of it. Councillor PESIBERION Shame. The DIRECToR : It in • shame. Continuing, he pointed out that even when free dinners were provided, the margin was not sufficient to keep the families leoently daring the whole week. There was no allow- ance for breakfast or tea. _ _

The hey. J. DAN 11C, asked how it was ti-at some able-bodied men received such low wages ? lie should like It to be an instruction from the committee to send so appeal to the employers of lal,nr fir the men'e wages to be raised. (Laughter.) The CHAIRMAN plated out that in many CAW the physical condition of the men presented them Bern- ina full wages. The SI A 1 On allied whether the principle mu sdrniqed that free education was to be taken as an excuse for free feeding ? It was • big queation. Why should there be free feeding in this respect more than in respect to other causes?

WI-XT.-DON : Because we don't get our money's worth.

Th-WCir Al RN( AN replied that the desirability had been laid down by the Board of Education of wing that no child went to sthool physically unfit. They had per- missive withority to spend a certain amount of money. Pitys roe, in replying to the discussion, raid be bad thought for pars thst the scale was too email. and

he hoped the sab-committee would very speedily take into consideration a wider scheme which would bring in a vast number of children who were not properly fed at the present moment. (Hear, hear.) When the com- mittee ceased to provide meals owing to the small number who availed themselves of them, they tried to get permission to issue tickets for use at a restaurant. The Board of Education would not, however, allow them to work on those lines: :

PAINTING THE COTTAGE HOMES.

The tender of Messrs. Parr and Hill, Orford-lane, Warrington, for painting the outside of the Cottage Homes, was accepted.

INCREASED RELIEF.

The CLERK submitted a return showing that the relief granted in the Warrington Union during the half year ended March 91st, 1911, showed an increase of £l2 as compared with the corresponding half-year. The Rev. A. M. MITCHELL asked how the increase in the expenditure was to be reconciled with a decrease in the number of recipients. According to an official return the average nurnter of persons relieved (in-door and out-door) on the Ist January, 1906-10 was 2,118, but on January Ist, 1911, it had fallen to 1810. Tbe number of indoor paupers had decreased from 817 to 809.

The CHAIRMAN : I take it the relief committees have been more libaral In the,r grants:to widows and children. Miss BROADBENT : This is so. The CLERK : You can rest assured that larger sums are being given now. , The Rev. A. M. MIToriELL : I am glad to bear of that liberality. I know it is suit Earlestown. Continu- ins, he asked whether the large decrease in the number of poor relieved might be attributed to the fact that in previous years the Guardians had been unduly extravagant and severity was now being exercised in certain directions of administration, or to better trade in Warrington. If the latter was the cause, it was a matter for congratulation all roJnd. Ile win wondering what beater.° of Mr. Jolley's prophecy 'hat large provision should be made in the House to meet the increase of population. There had been an increase of 14,0110 in he population, but there was, decline in the numbers of indoor and out- door poor. Therefore, he was afraid Mr. Jolley's prophecy had comets naught. (Laughter.) In face of the improved state of affairs, be hoped the Board would not persevere with their wild cat scheme for enlarging the dining hall &c. The extension was quite unneaeaeary all that was wanted being an expenditars of £350 or £4OO on the kitchen ores. Ile hoped that even at the eleventh hour they would repent, resolve to amend their ways, and avoid an expenditure of £5,000 or more. Mr. Jor.tav said he was bound to turn the douche of common sense on the rev. gentleman's impassioned out- burst. He felt tempted to quote Poe's lines about a little knowledge being a dangerous thing and to advise , Mr. Mitchell to "drink deep, or taste not the Pierian Ispring." (Laughter.) The statistics which Mr. Mitchell bad put before thorn and his (Mr. Jolley'a) prophecy were both right. If the rev, gentleman would take the trouble to digest his information before he placed it before the Board in a crude form, it would save time and be much better for everybody. He was certain Mr. Mitchell did not want to do anything that was unkind, harsh or unfair to the poor, but the policy which by as repeatedly championed was the old harsh, narrow policy. (The Bev. A. M. Mrrtirstt : "No, no.") It was no use aaying "No no " ; it was aia. A sensible scheme had been brought forward to improve the conditions of the indoor poor and the rev. ' gentleman bad done nothing but snarl and carp at it. The figures which he had quoted wi:h so much gusto related only .to a particular day, whereas the financial return extended over a period of six months. If the rev, gentleman had even an elementary kaowlege of the subject, be would have realised that and not made the exhibition of himself that he had done that morning. (Laughter.) The numbers of indoor poor were increas- ing. On a particular day they might happen to be lower than on the corresponding day twelve months before, but week after week there had 'reen a tendency for the numbers to increase. It was no use shutting their eyes to the facts. The wonder to him was that their expenditure during the half-year showed so alight an increase. Mr. Mitchell had no need to crow so soon, (Laughter). "10' TUAL ADMIRATION SOCIETIES."

Mr. JOLLEY presented a report on the 13th annual meeting of the Association of Poor Law Unions, recently held in London. The Rev. A. M. MITCHELL inquired if no reference was made to the reports of the Royal Commission on the Poor hew. The re!jorit; ead the minority reports recommended the abolition of the poor house and of such mutual admiration societies as Boards of Guardians (Laughter.) The CLERK said the Commission reported to Perlis- merit and it was for Parliament and not the Association of Poor Law Colons, to deal with the reports. Mr. JOLLEY said the reports had bees considered at I rrevious meetings of the association. They embodied an immense amount of research and tho cream of it bad already been carried into effect in changes in ' administration. Warrington stood in the front rank of progressive unions. There were over 600 Unions in the country and many of them were very much behind. They could not expect everything te move forward unanimously. Other people should try and come up to the Warrington standard. He did not think it could be justly said that the Board wee a mutual admiration society, judging by the criticisms occasionally passed from one side of the room to the other.(Laughter). Mr. FeRNES, : The admiration is all on one side. (Laughter).

THE PICTIIREDROME.

There are few people to whom the p'etorial does not make an appeal, and such a conclusion bee man obvious on making a visit to the Picturedroma, whose white wal's seem to always enclose a crowd. The management is in enterprising hands, and co effort is being spared to make the afternoon and evening enter- tainments not only strongly attractive but absolutely free fromanything of a vulgar or ohjeciionable nature. A wonderful programme is submitted to patrons this week including the great prize "Ambrosia" war film, which gives a series of pictures an realistic and animated that the cracking of rifles and the groans of the wounded seem to ring in one's ears. The war picture has a sentimental side, also, and this is as pretty as the other is grim. The amorous adventures of Henry of Navarre amid delightful scenery and N trman chateaux are the sub- ject of another entrancing series of pictures, while events of the day move before our eyes in steady procession. " Lost in the Jungle " is another triumph of the film-maker's art. Here we have a love affair, a quarrel, and the adventures in the African Jungle of a young Boer girt. Tropical trees wave before our eyes, a wild boar rambles through the undergrowth and in a second is at death gripe with a leopard. The ItOor girl also gets attacked by the deni.tens of the Jungle, but at the crucial moment is dramati- cally resetied by her father's elephant, said the cheers of the youngsters in Vie front seats of the picture-theatre. A comic leirglsr scene creates roars of laughter, and concludes a most I attractive programme. I From next Monday there will be matinees every afternoon at three o'clock, but every evening as in many large towns and cities, there ie t u bs a con- tinuous performance from six to 1030—an experiment which cannot fail to be successful.

THE EXAMINER.

SAMRDAY, NOVEMBER 25.1911

Mr. HAROLD SMITH'S MR. HAROLD history, we venture to SMITH'S say, is not his strong INACCURACIES. point and we suggest

that before he repeats his unfounded statements of Friday last week about the smashing of PARNELL it would be as well in the interests of all concerned if he consulted the actual facts of the case. His prophecy about the smashing of Mr. ASQUITH and Mr. REDMOND we may without fear or apprehension leave to the logic of coming events to no further period than the end of next year, but his sug- gestion that Home Rule was the cause of PARNELI:s " smashing" will cause amusement to students of modern history who reasonably expect a legislator to be a little more familiar with the salient facts of a period of history coinciding with his own lifetime. The man in the street will tell you that if Parnell could truly be described as " smashed " it was the want of Home Rule which led to that event, and that in an Ireland self-governed, even allowing for a Divorce Court decision, the close of the career of one who now stands as a Statesman of world-wide fame might well have been

changed. Strange again when Mr. SMITH comes to treat of modern Ireland, his description os four-fifths of the 'people of that country i the hard and ungenerous one of" disloyal." Does Mr. SMITH'S memory go back as far as KING GEORGE'S visit to Ireland? Does he recall the gratification which the KING and QUEEN on a quite recent occasion felt on the spontaneous welcome of their Irish subjects? What of the late KING EDWARD'S experiences in a country which Mr. Swill persists in misrepresenting!? On all sides it was admitted that no deeper sense of grief on the death of the late KIND was manifested than that which found expression in the ;ndividual and public mind of Ireland. The vast bulk of the Irish people have never wanted in loyalty to the Crown, just as they have never wavered in their disloyalty to the hideous system of government which stands condemned, root and branch, in the eyes of Europe. Contrast with this the supposed loyalty of the few in the north-east corner of Ulster. It is difficult to imagine, even allow- ing for the family weakness in the SmiThs for political "smartness,' how the people who invite tenders for rifles, arrange for pro- visional Government and talk about armed opposition to the forces of the KING and kicking the crown into the Boyne, should be

held up to English audiences as the '' loyal" minority. We see no reason to supposeithat , that game will succeed. Already it is being relegated to the limbo of exploded nostrums where now reposes the preposterous bogey of Separation. The line of attack is now to be that of -financial difficulties, which Mr. SMITH thinks insuperable. If he really holds that view he should never say another word about the greatness or the resources of an Empire on which "the sun never sets." For our part we welcome the criticism on finance, knowing as we do that if there be any argument more than another which is likely to appeal to your level-headed business-like Englishman it is what may be termed the £ s. d. side of the Home Rule question. It is the favourite cry just now in Tory circles to point to Ireland's bankruptcy. Where, we wonder, is the Tory's imagination or sense of humour, that he fails to perceive the Gilbertian situation of saying in one breath that the Government of Ireland on present lines is eminently success- ful and satisfactory and in the next glorying in the fact of that country's insolvency. Is it not rather humiliating to contemplate that after 111 years attempt to run the Irish Department of the British Empire the main leature about the system and the character- istic most gratifying to the supporters of that system is its bankruptcy ? The sane Englishman, we imagine, who looks at the question freely and without bias, will quickly realise that your Unionist orator can't have it both ways in the matter of Ireland's government and if that system shows a deficit then, in self-interest, to put it on no higher ground, it is imperative that a change in the methods of ruling that country should immediately be pressed forward.

Mr. Asquith's state- WOMAN SUFFRAGE. ment of the attitude of

the Government to the demands of the women's suffrage advocates would have been satisfactory, it might be , thought, to all the supporters of "Women's ' Votes." The senseless riots of Tuesdayl night, however, proved that there is still a militant section which refuses to accept the Prime Minister's concessions and would rather get nothing, if it cannot get its own way. Mr. Asquith stated (1) that the Government mean to carry, as far as in them lies, the Reform Bill for man- hood suffrage next year; (2) that it will be open to amend the Bill, so as to give the suffrage to women on different terms from men; (3) that the House of Commons is open to accept such an amendment; (4) that, if the amendment is carried, the Government will defend it as an integral part of the Bill.

Is the South Somerset SOUTH SOMERSET. result to be claimed by the Tories as a rebuff for the Insurance Bill? If so, then the Tories have at last nailed their anti-Insur- ance flag to the mast. Over the Old Age Pensions Bill they tried the same game of backing both horses, or rather trying to pre- vent the winning of one, and hedging so that .they could claim their winnings if their manceuvre proved unsuccessful. The case of -file out-workers has been used and abused by the Tory Party in South Somerset. On Tuesday Mr. Peel attacked the Chan- cellor of the Exchequer for sending a telegram which said that Mr. Peel's amend- ment to exclude out-workers altogether from the Bill was not supported by a single member. Mr. Peel said that he had not sent et counter-telegram accusing the Chancellor's 'statement as an utter travesty of the facts. But, although the South Somerset election had turned on tte exclusion of the out- workers, as Mr. Peel admitted, the only opportunity to exclude them, which came on Monday, did not produce a single vote from the Opposition to support Mr. Peel's amend- ment. Mr. Vivian is to be commiserated on his defeat by so small a majority after increasing the Liberal poll. Actually, he did 'wonderfully well against an opponent who had nursed the constituency through two previous elections, and there is some dissatis- faction with Liberal headquarters for giving Mr. Vivian so short a period in which to fight an uphill battle. He is a good man lost —though only temporarily, we hope—to the House of Commons!

A touch of gaiety has THE "MEANEST been lent to a serious

OF AGITATIONS." subject by the laughter- p r o vo k ing campaign which the "Daily Mail,"" Daily Telegraph," and other Tory organs are conducting against what they are pleased to call a servant tax. There are, it is true, possibilities of mischief in the orgy of misrepresentation in which a section of the Tory Press has been indulging —" one of the meanest of agitations," as Mr. Lloyd George justly characterised it. But the malicious purpose has been so palpable, the exaggerations so portentous, the impersonations of the average domestic by a too facile journalism so transparent, that the prevailing impression is one of comedy. Newspapers which have for years been the recipients of complaints about the "servant problem,r the discontent of the domestic, and the dissatisfaction of the mistress, are now apparently over-crowded with testimony. to the idyllic conditions which prevail in every household where servants are employed. A sober British public is asked to believe that, when a maid-servant informed that her mistress will be called

upon by the Insurance Bill to pay threepence per week in order to ensure her tendance and provision during sickness, she falls upon her mistress's shoulder and sobs the pathetic confession that, rather than allow her mistress to be bothered by a nasty Chancellor of the Exchequer who wants to make the kindest mistress in the world lick some nasty stamps, she will work hence- forward to the end of her days for nothing. If the correspondence columns of these hustling Tory journals could be regarded as indicating even in the slightest degree the real sentiment of the domestic-servant class, the Insurance Bill is going to save the mistresses of British households, as a class, thousands of pounds every year.

NOTES AND NOTIONS.

The police have been showing considerable activity against the street betting fraternity of late, but they landed a somewhat unusual catch on Wednesday, when a girl of 17 was fined £lO and costs for participating in the street-betting evil. She was caught receiv- ing betting slips and money from men and youths, and it is to be hoped that this early downfall may be a lesson to her.

• • The local police are not feeling altogether disappointed at not having a hand in the capture of Albert Wright, the desperado who broke into the Public Benefit Boot Company's shop on October 31st. Only two days later he was apprehended at Rishton and wounded two policemen with a revolver, while he had previously served six years for a similar dangerous offence. Justice Avory has now committed Wright for ten years. Shop- keepers and police alike will breathe more freely. • •

Some rather pertinent criticisms on the threatened boycott of the Insurance Bill by the doctors were made by the President of the local Druids on Thursday evening. His point is that the medicos are both greedy and unjust in demanding more than 6s. for the treatment of persons insured under the Bill, when for years past in Warrington and elsewhere the "club doctors" have been content with 3s. 6d. per head. * * *

The Wesley Historical Society has just published the first part of "John Wesley and Warrington'' xritten by our contributor Mr. Arthur Mounfield. As frontispiece a picture is given of Messrs. Tunstall's offices formerly "Heath House" and one of Wesley's Warrington homes. The modest building in Back Dallam-lane which was the first Methodist Chapel also finds itself raised to the unusual dignity of a share of the frontispiece. * •

We are glad to note that the prime. movers in the organisation of the Shake- spearian Society have taken last week's criticisms in such good part. Mr. Rossiter, the hon. sec., in another column elaborates a more practical programme than was out- lined at the inaugural meeting and invites the membership of those with either critical or suggestive ideas. There is a very promis- ing future before this newest of our local societies if run on really practical and helpful lines. * _ _ .

The Law List (a correspondent points out) contains thirteen solicitors named Porter,three Ginns,oneßeer,and a firm of"Ginn and Porter." There are also several Salts, Peppers, and Pickles, and one Peppercorn.. There is neither Brandy nor Seltzers; but one Brandy and two Seltzers appear in the London Post Office Directory. Another correspondent (Mr. Thomas Gurney) writes: I have come across the following in ancient records : " Hold - the-nest," " Clinkadagger," "Shakemaple," " Whiteacre," " Smallbeer," "Halfpenny," " Horsenail," and several more which would not look well in print. _ _

• Local antiquarians, Mr. Thomas May especially, will be gratified to find their labours at Wilderspool have brought our ancient " Veratinum ' before the notice of historians. In the most recent history of Cheshire, issued last week by the Clarendon Press, Mr. Charles E. Kelsey, M.A. gives to Wilderspool the very flattering title "The Birmingham of the Roman occupation." We quote the passage: The Romans taught the Britons many useful ' trades. Teratinum or Wilderspool became under the Roman quite a busy manufacturing town, the forerunner of a modern Warrington or Wigan. The site of the ancient Roman town has been carefully dm; over. Traces have been found of many pits, hearths, furnaces aLd ovens for the manufacture of glass and pottery, a bronze foundry, and an iron smelting furnace and an enameller's workshop. litany articles of iron, knives, padlocks, keys, nails, found en the same Fpot show that Veratinum was thel3irmingham of the Roman occupation." _ _ _

Mr. David Clegg will have the sympathy of his many Warrington admirers with the great loss which befel him by the destruction of his fine new organ during the recent gale. In a touching letter to the musical critic of the" Blackburn Times" Mr. Clegg says : " You would have wept to have seen the organ after the catastrophe. I had a very narrow escape. I had a recital to give in the afternoon, and about midday was playing away and testing the instrument when one of the blowers shouted 'Walls tumbling.' I rushed from the keyboard and fell on my face, luckily clear of the debris. When I looked round there was something like seven or eight tons of bricks and mortar where I had been sitting, burying my poor organ, my heart's work of the p& .t ten years gone in a minute. Now I have no heart at all, and I don't know what to do. However, on all sides I have the deepest sympathy, and that is something. One of the churches here are giving me the entire proceeds of a recital

I have been asked to give." Only the night before the unfortunate occurence Mr. Clegg 'gave a recital at Bold-street Wesleyan Church, Warrington, where on several previous occasions he had delighted large audiences by his wonderful mastery of the instrument. _

* * The under-fed school children are not to be neglected this winter, the Education Committee having resumed the provision of free dinners for those who come within the authority's standard of necessity. Mr. Upson was Justified in describing that standard— which allows an income of only 29. 6d. per head—as ludricously low and we hope early steps will be taken to revise it. In the meantime there is some satisfaction in knowing that the most necessitious cases are not being allowed to suffer, and some credit is certainly due to the Trades and Labour Council for their energetic efforts in arousing public opinion on the question. * * There was a lively exchange of compli- ments in our local Parliament on Tuesday evening 'when members began to call in question the• views of the Member for War- rington—meaning friend Harold—on certain aspects of the Electoral Reform Bill. Some heat was, of course, engendered but the Speaker tactfully relieved the situation with the remark: "The only Member for War- rington that we can recognize is Mr. E. , England." Unfortunately, however, the absence of Mr. England robbed the House of that authoritative pronouncement with which he would no doubt have been only too willing to oblige.

The latest returns of the population—or rather depopulation—of l ranee are surpris- ing. For some years the population has been almost stationary. But now, for the first time, the balance is on the other side, and the numbers of the living are falling. Ireland is perhaps the only other European country that has shown a diminishment in its inhabitants. But that was due to emigra- tion which was imposed by insistent starva- tion, France is wealthy, and with wealth spread as it is spread in no other European country. And Frenchmen do not emigrate in any appreciable numbers. It is neither starvation nor emigration that is responsible for the depopulatian of France. The bald fact is that for the first half of 1911 the deaths exceeded the births by 18,279. Almost equally startling is the decline in our own natural increase of population for the third quarter of the year. The increase is more than 40,000 less than in any of the three pre- ceding years—equal to a fall in the rate of increase of over 30 per cent. There is a decline in the birth-rate which calls for some explanation. .

The figures as to the import of milk are of importance only as showing how very quickly milk would flow in from abroad if the price were to rise here. It would be just as easy to send fifty thousand hundred- weights as it is to send five thousand. From the point of health the matter wants looking ' into, because, with an increased surveillance as exercised over the production of the home article, there is no means of ensuring cloanliness in the countries from which milk is imported, and it would be a laborious task to subject every consignment to microscopic examination, yet that alone would satisfactorily demonstrate the presence or absence of the germs of disease. That fresh milk was sent across the Channel to Great Britain in such a very hot summer proves that the trade might conceivably be established, and the English farmers would do well to meet it at the outset by increasing the quality of their own supply. It would be a pity for them (says "Country Life" if they were to lose the monopoly at present enjoyed.

Church Society Lecture. The second of the series of lectures arranged by the Warrington Church Sooiety was given in St. Clement's Mission, Bank-street, last evening, by the Rev. L. J. T. Darwall, vicar of Walton who spoke on " Litnrgial music." Be pointed out that liturgia' music was distinct from "church " or" sacred " music inasmuch as it was music which was in itself worship, and its only real purpose was in connection with the central rite of our religion. He first devoted attention to two periods (1) when plainsong alone was used ; (2) when plainsong was retained for certain portions, while others might be song to measured music. These lastrovere six in number (1; the Kyrie ; (2) the Gloria in If.xcelsis ; (3) the Credo; (4) the Sanctus ; (5) the Bendictus ; (1) the Agnns Dei. The iev. gentleman gave an interesting explanation of plainsong, and next touched upon Palestrina and polyphonic music. Coming to the English Reforma- tion, he said it was succeeded by the disappearance of plainsong and the decline of all Eucharistic music except in a few places. Abroad, the pclyphonic school was superseded by the harmonic (save in connection with the Papal choir). Allusion was made to the Masses of Bach, Haydn and Mozart. Finally, the , lecturer dealt with the further development of sensuous and dramatic elements in the Masses of Rossini, Gounod and others—The interest of ths lecture was increased by a number of musical illustra- tions efficiently given by a party of vocalists from Walton. The items included :—From Palestrins's Maas :Darns Christi Manera " (a) Kyrie (b) Agnus Del ; from Mozart's First Mass (a) Agnes Del, solo, Miss Dorothy Truslove ; (b) Dona Nobis; from Rossini's "Mesae Solennelle," (a) Domini) Deus, solo, Mr A Greenough ; (b) 0 Salutaris, solo, Mr R W Cook; from Gonna "Mesas • Solennelle," Agnns Dei.

St. John's Presbyterian Guild. Tbe members of the above Society met on Monday , evening in the Municipal Museum to hear a lecture on " Warrington in the Stuart Period," by Mr. C. Madeley.—ln ope, ing, the Lecturer pointed out that the chief object was t illustrate the history of this pet iod by the rations articles that bad from time to time been gathered together at tLe Museum. The general appearance of the town wa described and illustrated by a painting of the Old Court House which occupied the site now filled by the Market Hall, and reference was made to the old buildings still existing that dated back to the 17th century. Many local incidents of the Civil War and the great religious controversies of the reriod were recalled by extracts from books and pamphlets yellow with age. Specimers were shewn of the tokens introduced by private traders to supply the need of small change, prior to the introduction nf copper coinage, and many interfering relics of the Siege of Warrington in the shape of cannon balls, etc. Altogether a most dflightful evening ens spent, and at the closes cordial vote of thanks was accorded Mr. Madelry for the pleasure and instruction Riven.

At the Warrington County Court on Thursday, before his Honour Judge Stanger, K C., the hearing was concluded of the action in which Allston Morland Johnson, electrical engineer, Manchester, sued J. Bayley Isaac, provision dealer, Warrington, fQr £42 13s. 3i for personal injuries and damages to a motor cycle alleged to have been caused by the defendant's negligent driving of a motor car. Mr. Suffer, of Manchester (instructed by Mr. LW. H. Todd, Manchester), appeared for the plaintiff, and the defendant was represented by Mr. Cuthb rt Smith, of Liverpod (instructed by Messrs. Wood and Lord, of Manchester). The action was a sequel to a collision at the Delamere cross roads and plaintiff alleged

that the car was being driven at too great a speed to enable it to pull tip. In attempting to turn to the right, the car collided with the porch of an old toll house, broke one of the pillars and did other damage—The defendant contended that he took every precaution and sounded his horn, but the plaintiff was travelling so rapidly that a collision was inevitable. The car was travelling at between four and five miles an hour. After the accident plaintiff was alleged to have admitted that it was his fault and to have eaid to the defendant " You saved my life by doing what you dbl." Defen- dant's version of the affair was borne out by Messrs George Finch, licencee of the Griffin Hotel, and F. Milling who were travelling in the car.— His Honour, in giving judgment, raid it was incumbent on both parties to have used proper caution when approaching such a dangerous crows road. He thought the plaintiff was guilty of some imprudence in increasing his speed when the other car was in view or Wore the car came into view. In his evidence be said be increased the speed and it was that act which debarred him from succeeding in this action. The plaintiff might have avoided the accident by exercise of reasonable care. He gave judgment for the defendant with coits.

Burroughs and Wellcome always send acme good slides when a society asks ior a yearly lecture, belt on Tuesday an exceptionally good lecture accom- panied them. Although, as is natural, the reading matter deals largely with the excellence of of their products in their own eyes, it was in many parts an inspiration to an amoteur photographer. Its title, "Ways and Means in Photography," was well borne out by the hints as to where and how to get our pictures, and in a more strictly Parliamentary sense, it gave the ways and means of making some money by our hobby, and eves broad hints as to how to spend it when made. The writer spent a holiday at Robin Hood's Bay and during a storm a steamer was driven ashore and became a complete wreck. With a tele- scopic lens some excellent pictures of the ruined vessel half hidden by the spray were taken next morning, and a last view of the fragments of hull left on the shore were pathetic enough. The crew had been rescued by the rocket apparatus. The fifty pictures in the lecture included views of Bye and Winchelsea as well as .ther bits from all over the country and even from Alt ica. GARLAND, Ironmonger, Bridge It., Warrington.—Advt. Warrington Socialist Society. The weekly lecture in connection with the above society, was held on Sunday evening last. Mr. Harry Hughes of St. Helens se Ate on "The practicability of S He said it was a lamentable fact that the workers accepted and resigned themselves to miserable conditions as though they were inevitable and impossible of being changed. The worker regarded S cialism as a dream and therefore impossible of attainmeet. Socialism was first and foremost a "bread and butter" question, and seeing that it undoubtedly would affect the economic standing of every worker, it should recieve their serious con- sideration. The profound c ,ntradiction in modern society was due to the fact that the principle of co-operation was invariably employed for production, whereas the distribution of the profim was made solely upon the principle of individualism. Public ownership would inevitably alter this. As to the practicability of public ownership, abundant favour- able proof is continually forthcoming. Until this idea was extended to include all means of production and the peop'e had complete power and control enabling them to ieceive full return fcr their labour, nothing could benefit them to any considerable extent. A drawing-rrom meeting, in connection with the above, was held at the Hollins, Latchford, by invita- tion of Mrs. Broadb:nt, the object being to elect a Ladies' Auxilia7 Committee for Warringtou acd district, to work in conjunction with the General Com- mittee, of which Mr. E. (Hear, hear.) In answer to accusations sometimes heard that the Institution was extravagant in administration. Mr. Johnson said that oily seven per cent. f the funds was used in this way. During 1910 the number of lives saved by the National Lifeboat was 767, and since the Institution was started, in 1824, the number was 19,394. A striking incident in connection with the meeting is the fact that Mr. Morris himself, and the son of one or the ladies present, had been saved from wreck in the same lifeboat.

SENSATIONAL AMALGAMATION. MESSRS. CROSFIELDS' ABSORBED BY MESSRS. BRUNNER MONO'S.

The news reached us last evening of a sensational development in commercial cirelso by which Messrs. Brunner, Mond, and C 0,,, Ltd., the world-famous chemical manufac- turers of Northwich and Winsford, have absorbed Messrs. Joseph Crosfield and Sone. Ltd., soap and chemical manufacturers, Warrington.

We ai'occe telephoned Mr. J. F. L. Brunner for confirmation of the report, and he very courteously informed us that it was quite correct. He added in explanation that the two companies had exchanged shares, and that the preponderating influence would be with Messrs. Brunner Mond and Co.

STATEMENT BY MR. J. J. CROSFIELD. Last evening we telephoned Mr. J. J. Crosfield, chairman of Messrs. Joseph Crosfield and Sons, Ltd, who informed us that the amalgamation would make no difference to the conduct of that firm's busi- ness, which would be carried on under the same management as at present. "We have always" be added" had very close business relations with Messrs. Brunner Mond & Co., and are their biggest customers. This is simply drawing the ties a little closer."

GOSSAGES' INCLUDED. Later we learned that the firm of Messrs. Gossage, of Widnes, was also included, making the amalgamation one of the largest which has taken place in the commercial world for a long time. There can be little doubt that the new move will be a good thing for Warrington, as the association of these leading firms presents a tremendously powerful combin- ation for developement purposes. Warrington will, it is safe to assume, participate largely in those extensions. The enterprising character of Messrs. Brunner, Mond & Co., is well-known—in fact the firm has a world- wide reputation, and is one of the best or 'employers. Each of the three concerns will preserve its separate identity as at present, and each will strengthen the other in certain directions.

Death of Mr. George Mounfield. We much regret to announce the death Yesterday of Mr. George Mounfield, Ingle Nook, Latchiord. Mr. Mounfield wan confined to his bed with cancer of the throat at the time of the lamented death of his wife on September IS h last, and be has gradually worsened ever since. He was well-known in business as cabinet-maker and undertaker in Buttermarket- street, and enjoyed the respect and esteem of a large community. Mr. Mounfield was about 61 years of age, and bad long been associated with the Friars- green Independent Ali thodist Church, and later with the Independent Me hodiats at Gtappenhall. EXTRAORDINARY STORY.

An extraordinary rose was opened at the Warrington Police Court on Tuesday when a charge of stealing furniture to the value of £BO wasnreferred against John Woosey. an sx-rail way porter, of Parker-street, Warring- ton, and Elias Emily Partridge, 6 White-street. The furniture, which included a rosewood cabinet, was the property of Thomal Partridge, the huaband of the male defendant Mr. A. Browne, who prosecuted, evplained that Mr. Partridge wee a post office linesman, stationed at Warrington. Since July last the prisoner Woolsey bad been payina great attention to Mrs. Partridge. In con- sequence numerous quarrels bad taken place, and the climax was reached on Friday morning last when Mrs. Partridge threatened she would leave her husband. He warned her that if she did go she must not take any- thing with her, to which the replied she would take the whole lot. The husband, thinking it was an idle threat, went to his work as must. at eight o'clock the next morning. On his return, he found the place in total darkneaa, and that the house had been stripped of the furniture. tie made inquiries and ascertained that the two prisoners bad been loading the furniture on a railway lorry during the day. He traced the furniture to Bank Quay. Goods Yard, and found that it had been despatched to Edgebill Station, Liverpool A search was made for the prisonere, bat without success. On Monday Police Sergeant Stuart was waiting near the Edge-hill Goods Station, where he saw both prisoners and effected their arrest. He (Mr. Browne) applied for a remand, as the magistrates had no jurisdiction to try the case. He was prepared to prove that the articles were the sole property of the husband g A remand of seven days was granted.
