MRS. BOOTH LECTURES ON " SCHUBERT."

A delightful evening was provided for the members of Bold-street Wesley Guild on Monday when Mrs. Arthur Booth gave a paper on " Franz Schubert," followed by illustrations from his works by Mrs Booth, Miss Clarice Newell and Mr. W. Hipkiss. In a most interesting way Mrs. Booth outlined the career of the great musician, pointing out that from his earliest years Schubert, who Won a native of Vienna, showed a great love of music. While still very young be contracted a warm boyish friendship for a young relative—a joiner'sapprentice—and often went with him to visit a pianoforte warehouse,where he was employed. There the young musician learnt his (fist exercises without any teacher to guide ban and ssbenat the age of I seven, he commenced a course of regular musical instruction, it was discovered that he had already mastered all the rudiments which his master proposed to teach him. Not only as an execntant did he cause' his friends surprise, for at a very early age he began to pour out the divine melodies with which his soul over- flowed, Perhaps in the whole of musical history we should find that, with the exception of Mozart, there was soother prodigy who could be said to compare with Schubert. Mozart was laded 1 an infant wonder, writing s concerto for the piano at the age of six. But after Moz at, there was earl none equal to Schubert. His brother Ferdinand told Us that Franz wrote a fantasia for both hands for the piano at the age of thirteen, and " The Lament of Hagar "—his first song—at the age of fourteen. But there was evidence that he had written songs, pieces for the piano- forte, and even string quartets, long before then. Though Schubert had composed so many other forms of work, it was essentially as a song writer that he would always be remembered. When he was fairly lunched on his musical career, he discovered, as so many had done before and since, how difficult it was to obtain recognition and recompense for ertistic labours. Could we name any great composer whose genies had been filly recognised in his life-time ?Some had had recognition, hut wanted bread. Others had had food, but no recognition. Schubert was without both. Like Mozart and Beethoven, he had a great aversion to teaching music, and excepting in one instance, always avoided that means of earning a livelihood. The lectuer next touched upon the one romantic episode of Schubert's life his unrequited love for the Countess Caroline. Soloubert never actually declared his passion, but it was undoubtedly revealed to her indirect ways, though it was possible she never realised its strength and fervour. Reference was made to the composer's struggles and disappoint- ments, his bad health and poverty which at one time seemed to be driving him to despair. In a letter dated 1824 he wrote t " Every night when I go sleep, I hope that I may never wake again, and every morning renews the grief of yesterday. My affairs are going on badly and I never have any money." Soon afterwards he had his first success with some of his songs at a concert. Eventually, he accepted £BO from the publishers for the copyright of twelve songs, including the " Erl King." But 113 far as the lecturer could discover, that was the largest fee ever paid to him by any publishers. Toe Eel King" alone brought them in £2,700, which made it not a bad speculation for the publishers. That Schubert wastreated badly by his publishers was evident. Even in his later years, when his name was becoming known, he found it quite impossible to procure any satisfactory negotiations with them. Perhaps in some respects it was his own fault. It was a well-known fact that the artistic nature was inclined to be untusibeeslike, and in addition Schubert had an innate modesty that made him reserved and timid to such a degree that he failed to reap the harvest of his labours. That his finances were indeed in a low state might be gathered from the fact that when he died Is November 1828, before he bad completed his 32nd year, the whole of his property, including his clothing, was officially valued £6. And it was necessary for his friends to give a concert, and collect friendly offering., to enable them to place a monument on his grave. When we con- sidered the gigantic mass of compositions left by him, and the early age at which death overtook him, we must be convinced of his extraordinary industry and activity. A great talent was committed to his c ire, and surely he utilized it faithfully and nobly. Though no little recognized during his life, no student would deny that he held an important place in the history of music. It was he who created for us the art song as we know it. Before Schubert the scheme of song as a setting for poetry was tune, and tone only, but he introduced into his songs a realism which had welded into a perfect 'whole the living words and mnsic. Delightful hours might be spent with his beautiful melodies, in trying to read into them some of the meaning be had expressed there. And the time spent must assuredly be much more profitably occupied than in wading through some of the wishy washy sentimental ballads one heard so often in the modern drawingroom. The musical programme which,along with the lecture, was highly appreciated was as follows:—Planoforte solo "Impromptu wiith variations in Bfiat," Miss Clarice Newell : songs ,(1) "Who in Sylvia," (h) "Hark, hark, the lark," (c) Margaret at the spinning wheel," (d) "The EH King," Mrs. Booth ; pianoforte nobo "Impromptu in E flat," Miss Clarice Newell; song "The Shepherd on the Bock" (clarinet obligato by Mr. Hipkin), Mrs. Booth. Miss Newell was the accompanist.

Wycliffe Ch arch. Foreign Missionary Anniversary.

Sermons on behalf of foreign missions were preached at Wycliffe Congregational Church on Sunday, when there were large congregations. In the morning the pastor (Rev .1 Yonge) made a strong appeal for support for the London Missionary Society, giving interesting particulars as to the amounts raised for that object during his pastorate at Wycliffe. In the evening the Rev W C Willoughby, of Tiger Kloof. S. Africa, gave an interesting and powerful address on work in that psrt of the missionary field. The colllections for the day amounted to £52 17s. Ed. In connection with the Anniversary a missionary meeting was held in the Memorial 11411 on Wednesday evening at which the principal speakers were: the Rev W C Willoughby, of South Africa and the Rev U Collins of Madagascar. Mr. COLLINS, who has been engaged in missionary work for nearly thirty years, gave an interesting account of hie eight years work in South Madagascar. He said the people of that part of the country found intercession in the worship of their ancestors. They pleaded with their ancestors to propitiate between them and God. He had seen families gathered round the tomb of their ancestors and pray for the spirits to intercede This worship had led the people to resort to the wear- ing of charms. There was a belief in witchcraft and the people were entirely under the influence of the witch doctors and diviners. Despite these conditions the Caristien missions had made great p.ogreas. Thirty Tears ago they had only one Sunday school, but now they had 597 with attendance of 30,00) scholars. They had also a number of day schools, but the French regulations prevented them teaching certain subjects. The missionaries did a great deal of good work is dis- pensing medicines. The people were full of ignorance in regard to the question of health and if the mission- aries only continued to do that kind of work they would have accomplished something indeed. Notwithstand- ing the ninety years missionary work in Madagascar two-thirds of the population was still without any know- ledge of Jesus Christ, The Rev. W. C. WiLLOCCHBY said that the states which formed the British Empire were linked together, not by any Imperialistic bond but by the b nad of a common ideal. It was their duty as Nonconformista to take religion into the furthermost pert of the Empire. Their message was one of individual responsibility, and in taking that message to the nation they avers in danger of promoting a parochial spirit. Parochialism became a distinctive danger to Nonconformity which found its distinctive impressions in egotism. The only possible way to counteract that danger was to constantly worship at the cross which woe the great symbol of sacrifice. What they wanted to secure was the allvation of the whole world. It was often said that charity began at home. They had heard it no often that they were not likely to forget it, bui, if it stayed there it was not christian charity. Christianity had only survived by being eggressivo and wires it ceased its aggression the dnadisnce which threatened will complete the end. It was the missions which gave birth to that comparative religion, and it was one of the moral contributions which the missions had made to the church at hc me. (Applause.)

Latchford Wesleyan Church.

Mr. Poenett accompanied by his sister and Miss Harris took up missionary labours at Medak seventeen years ago and the fact that they were able to open a dispensary immediately on their arrival was of infinite importance. The very famine which was such a cares to the country generally provided a splendid object lesson to the people of the reality of the Christian doctrines of love and service for their fellow men. Me., Posnett was able to unfold a romantic, wonderful and thrilling story of one of the most successful medical and evangelistic missions in the whole mission field. Referring to native agencies, be spoke of the value of the theological training institution which had been established. It was absolutely necessetry that their evangelists in the first place should be " men of one book," acql he bad striven with evangelists and Bible women all these years to make it their ideal. The type of men who came to them now was miles ahead, both

SPIRITUAL Re:LIGION AND SOCIAL REFORM.'

-- On Monday evening the Rev. F, J. POPE delivered • lecture on " Spiritual Religion and Social Reform." Mr. R. CORBETT, who presided, said there was a great need throughout the country for more spiritual religion. When they got that, they would have a greater measure of social reform, and of that true reform which resulted from the individual being spiritualised The Rev. P. J. POPE said many people had the idea that in the two phrases" Spiritual religion " and '• Social reform " they had two schemes with nothing in common, two different ways of dealing with the woes, the wrongs and the eins of men. On the one band they had the man to whom religion wan such a spiritual thing that it had nothing whatever to do with social movements and had no place whatever in the political world. It was a private thine belonging to a man's inner life. On the other hand they had the man whose motto was "Social reform," who had no patience with anything that was speculative or visionary, who raid there might be a God or there might not, bat our principal business was with the world as we found it and with our fellows. He would nay" Look around you and you will see men living under conditions that make morality and goodness almost impossible. You will see how one man trades upon another; you see suffering, wrong and injustice on every hand. The proper thing to do is to take off your coat and try and do something to right those wrongs and make the world a better and happier place. Let us look after our brothers and, if therein a God in heaven, it will be all right at the last." He (the speaker) was there to publish the banns of marriage between spiritual religion and social reform. He wished to take his hearers through certain periods of the history of our country to show how, over and over again, it had been demonstrated that spirituel religion and social reform were linked together in the purpose of God. If they read history as John Richard Green wrote it, they began to under- stand its fascination. When he (the speaker) went to school, they lone to wade in blood from the first to the last page of history and, except for the names of Binge and Queens and a few dates, it was mostly a record of battles they had to learn. He wee glad we 'bad come to have a saner and truer view of English history and John Richard Green represented the new way of interpretative it. He declared that wars had played a very small part in the development of our country and the building up of our character and constitution, and that other things—peaceable movements, literature and preaching—had, more than anything else, helped to make England great. The speaker referred to the condition of serfdom in whnti large masses of the Engliah people found themselves after the Norman conquest tallith pieced the country under new management and gave all the high places in the State and Church to Normans. Theo he passed on to the signing by King John of the Magna Charts, which lay at the root of our liberties. That Charter was conceded not merely through the pressure of the Barons who gathered round the king and forced him to sign it, but bemuse England at large demanded it. In the words of Stubbs, the constitutional historian, the vest Charter was the Act of the united nation—the Church, the barons and the commons for the first time thoroughly at one." What wes reaposible for the creation of that new national spirit and the enact- ment of that magnificent social advance? John Richard Green said that one of the mightiest influences was a great religions revival under the preaching of the Cistercian monks, which bad lifted up lowly men and women and given them a sense of their dignity and rights. The lecturer next touched upon a period when the religious life of England had sunk down to a very low state. The monks had become rich, bad lost their spiritual fervour, and religion had become a mere formality. The dying embers were, however' re-kindled by the Franciscan and Dominican orders of friars, poor men who spread themselves amongst the people, preaching and doing all kinds of coc.al work. There was again created in the common people a sense of independence and one of the revolts was anew democratic spirit which stirred the industrial classes to assert a share is the municipal administration which had hitherto been confined to wealthier members of the merchant guilds. Later Simon de Montfort extended representative government by summoning not only the bishops, lords and landed aristocracy but representatives of the citizens of each borough to Parliament. The next phase to come under the lectureee review was the preaching of John Wycliffe and the Lnllards—another mighty spirittal movement with wonderful effects on the lives of the common people. Trade unionism was born in a spiritual move- ment. The Lellards formed unions amongst the peasants and collected money which they held for distribution in time c need. With the "Black Death " which visi' ad England about that time came a shortage of labour, with a consequent rise in wages. An unjust and oppressive act was then passed, called the Statute of Labourers, which revived the old rule of feudalism under which no man was permitted to go beyond the limits of his manor in search of work, nor should he receive higher wages than were paid before the plague broke out. The Act, however, became a dead letter because the masses who had been converted by the preaching of Wycliffe and his followers, had epirit enough to refuse to recognise it as binding. The belief that they were sons of God and, as such, had their rights was the one great force in breaking up the feudal system and granting the people liberty to labour where work could beet be found and to make the best terms they could. Lastly the speaker described in glowing terms the spiritual revival of which John Wesiev was the head and which had effects more far-reaching than any other revival in thie country. John Wesley, under the hand of God, was able to save the England of the 18th century, which bad sunk into torpor, indifference and the most glaring iniquities. The workers received miserably trifling wages and the prices of commodities were much higher then in these days. One in every five of the population was thrown upon the parish in old age. Wesley was not simply an evangelist; he did a variety of work which was almost bewildering. He formed a loan society, opened dis peussries for the treatment of the poor, and issued a great quantity of cheap and good literature. Out of the Methodist revival of the Ilids century, the democracy of the 39th century took its rise. It created the missionary work of that century which stood out in history as the missionary century. There I was nothing finer on earth than the arrangements I for the Methodist ministry. No Methodist minister I could ever get rich or starve. The most brilliant, gifted end powerful man in their ministry stood pretty much on a level with the humblest. poorest and meanest, from a financial point of view. That was a bit of real Christiaa Socialism. Wesley created the modern social movement, and many a reform which had meant the uplifting of the poor and the conferring of rights upon the humble, would never have come to England if it had not been for the splendid impulse of the Methodist revival. It was his (the speaker's) con- viction that without the strengthening, purifjing, sweetening influence of trua spiritual religion they could never gain any strong, abiding measure of social reform. It was ths business of the ehole Church to revive in our midst religion of the true Scriptural type—a religion that would express itself in practical sympathy with the wrong and oppressed and create such a public opinion and spirit of unselfishness us RlAlltd mate possible many practical measures to uplift the poor, relieve the distressed and make the earth • little bit more like the paradise of God. (Applause). _ _ Chapter

Claim Against a Warrington Auctioneer.

WARRINGTON CHAPTERS.

A COLLAPSE AND A TRIUMPH. A FINE METHODIST- STORY TOLD BY THE LATE MR JOB SWALES

Old lArarringtonians will need no descrip- tion of the late Mr. Job Swales, and those who knew him most closely will read with deep pleasure, though not with surprise, the story of his first sermon. It is a happy thing that the narrative has been preserved and we give it here as told by the central figure. We believe that no detail has been altered and it is in every way true to the Methodist life of two generations ago. Risley Chapel still stands though it fell into disuse last year after the erection of the new buildirg at Fearnhead.

MY FIRST SERMON. "Most persons who speak in public retain through life vivid recollections of their first attempt. Even those who possess strong nerves and whose training has fitted them for public sp2aking know something of the peculiar sensation experienced when deliver- ing their maiden speech. The young man who was going to thrill a temperance meeting by his eloquence, and who early in the morning addressed himself to the cabbages in his father's garden did very well—in the garden. But when he rose at the meeting and beheld the mass of human faces bzfore him be sat down after he had tremblingly uttered the first two sentences of his elabor- ately pr.mared speech. To the untrained and uneducated youth public teaching is especially hard, and it was to this class that I belonged. My parents deserted me when a baby and I was brought up at a charitable school. From this place I was taken by a farmer as an indoor apprentice, and led a life like that of a slave. But my master insisted upon my attendance at chapel and in after years I respected him for this. As a youth I detested chapel, but my conversion changed all that, and as time went on I became a Sunday school teacher. The chapel folks, however, were not content to let me alone in my labours. They said I ought to begin to preach the Gospel. The very mention made me tremble. I declined again and again feeling quite unequal and unworthy. But the circuit meeting took me in hand, and, following the Methodist custom, appointed me to accompany one of the old preachers to give out hymns and in other small ways help in the service. I received the information with astonishment; I shook so that I could ccarcely stand, but I dare not say nay. The S-Jnday on which I was to first go out with my old friend was a beautiful morning in June. I arrived at his house to find him seriously ill and unable to rise from his bed. He told me I must go to Risley and take the services without him, for the people must not be disappointed. "Go myself, Go myself" I repeated as "

passed out of the ouse. "Go myself" said as I stood in the street, like one in a dream. But this time there was an echo in my heart" My presence shall go with thee." I started on my journey and was soon under leafy trees where the birds sang loudly, and where blossoming hedgerows gave fragrance to the air. I found the Chapel at the junction of two roads, its walls adorned with honeysuckle and white roses. Passing through the gate I met a man with a straw hat and without coat, to whom I told my errand. He looked me over from head to foot and then with a jerk of the thumb said "Goo in." I entered with trembling limbs, and not daring to look around walked straight to the pulpit there falling on my knees in prayer, the tears streaming down my cheeks the while. On rising I ventured to look around. There were about thirty persons present, men, women and children. Most of the men were without coats and the women without bonnets, but all were remarkably clean. Right in front of me sat a man of about fifty years of age. He had a square-looking solemn face, and grey, dull eyes, which I noticed fixed on me. Somehow from the first look at the man I dreaded him. There was a sinister expression about his mouth I did not like. I gave out the hymn, and the people sang heartily. During prayer many a hearty " Amen " told me that there were earnest Christians there who entered into the service with gladness. I read the Scriptures and we sang another hymn. Then came the most trying moments I ever knew. I had chosen as text " Him that cometh unto Me I will in no wise cast out." For a few sentences I went on all right, but chancing to look at the square- faced man I thought he was merring at me. Like a flash all my ideas vanished, my mind was a perfect blank, I could not utter a single word. There I stood, a cold chill running through my body, and a cold sweat standing out in large drops on my face. Someone made an encouraging exclamation, then all was quiet. The sparrows chirped on the eaves, the bees hummed in at the open door, and I wished I were a bird or a bee that I could fly away. How long I stood I cannot tell. To me

seemed an hour. Then I saw the square- faced man open his lips and I heard his loud voice say: "Well, young mon, if tha's done, tha's done; sit thee deawn." I sat me down and covered my face with my hands. The flood-gates of my soul were opened aud I wept like a child. I know not how long I wept but I was arcused from my grief by a touch on my arm, and looking up I saw an aged woman who said in a kindly voice: _ .

"Come, my lad, come wi' me for a bit o' dinner." "I would rather go back home" I said. "say, nay, my lad" said the good woman, "that'll never do; the cievil would laugh if thou ran whoam. Come here's thee hat, come wi' me." "1 rose from the seat, and was astonished to find the chapel quite empty; all the people excepting ourselves having gone away. I was glad of this for I dreaded to meet their gaze. But the ordeal had to come. Outside they stood in groups along the road and this is what we heard as we walked. "That him. Owd Betty's getten him eawt at last." "Ay poor chap. He'd better a stopped a whoam." "He's ony a young un " pleaded a woman's voice "An I'm sure he started off very well." "Some folks will put their self; i places theyre noan fit for" said the same voice that had bid me "Sit thee deawn." " An empty baecanno stand up" chimed another. I felt all this very keenly but Old Betty buoyed me up. "Never heed um,lad ; they'd make a sorry mess on it their eels if they tried their hand." Betty was a widow and lived alone in a neat tiny cottage. "I've flout extra for

dinner lad" she said " but thou'rt welcome," and I saw a basin of new milk and some brown bread before me on the table. "I never cook on Sundays; I think Sundays belong to the Lord." . .

After we had been eating a few minutes she stopped short and said: "What made thee break down so suddenly. I know there was summat for thou was goin' on nicely" and she looked at me with a smile.

"Well to tell the truth that man who sat right in front of me frightened me."

" What old Amos?" "I don't know his name but he has some- thing about his face that I don't like and I was timid." _ _ _

------ " But thou wasn't preachicg to please him I hope." "No, no, I was trying to please God," I said.

Then Betty broke out "That's the sort o' preaching we wants. Bless thee my lad, as soon as ever I heard thee speak I felt fain, for summat said inside me 'God has sent

him,' and I was praying for thee all the tim-. ~The devil has all macks o' ways o' bulkitcw folks as are trying to do good,' and may-he he did sneer out o' old Amos's face; but I'll tel thee what thou mun do to-night." " What " I exclaimed for Betty had made my heart beat quick. Why, my lad, just knock the devil off his perch. Take the word of God and throw it at him. Jesus said 'Get thee behind me Satan,' and thou mun pray to God to give thee a word in season. I'll pray, too, and if we don't shift th'old lad from his perch it'll be our fault and not God's."

We spent the afternoon in reading the Bible and quiet talk, and as we got ready to I go to chapel in the evening Betty's advice was Pray a good deal, lad, and then -thou'll preach well." While we had been so quiet in the cottage there had been stirring things going on in the village. The news had spread of my breakdown, and everyone who scoffed at the Methodists decided to come to the chapel to see the fun of a second catastrophe. The whole population seemed to be there and it was only by an effort that Betty and I could get in. Every corner was packed and several men were flattening their noses on the windows outside to get a look at me. I begun with the hymn "Rock of Ages" and noticed as we sang it that Betty had found a corner behind the door and was sitting with her face covered as though in earnest prayer. I stood up to preach without a tremor, and took the text I had taken in the morning. As I read i I saw the people look at each , other and smile as though to say " Now for it." And it came, but not in the manner they expected. Strange to say, though it was my first real sermon I spoke with perfect ease, and as I did so a hush fell upon all. Many began to weep and some of the tears were tears of repentance. The service closed with every evidence of deep feeling and when I announced a prayer meeting to follow about forty people remained. It was a memorable time; some wept, some prayed, some sang. One man had flung himself upon the ground and was in tears. Seeing he was deeply moved I touched him on the shoulder. He raised his face from the ground and to my astonishment I saw it was the face of old Amos. Pis voice was choked as he said " Eh, lad but I connot live r this state. Tell me what aw mun do." What's amiss Amos ?" I asked. "Amiss, amiss" he cried " everthing's amiss; aw fhel as if aw the sins at ever aw did were up a top o' me, an aw connot bear urn." "Throw 'em at the foot of the Cross" I cried. Amos got rid of his burden that night, and lived to be an earnest Methodist worker. When I shook hands with old Betty that night she said with tears of joys in her eyes "Didn't aw tell thee God had sent thee? He let thee fail this mornin to get the sinners to chapel to fleet. The Lord bless thee lad." A. M.

FROM LABOUR'S STANDPOINT.

By Philip Snowden, I P.

THE ATTACK ON TRADE UNIONISM. The recent Labour troubles have thoroughly frightened the capitalists. Trade Unionism is going to have to fight for its existence. Capitalists are organising for a great attack upon Trade Unionism, When the next strikes takes place it will be found that the employers are not so unprepared as they were last summer. A good deal of quiet preparation and organisation is going on; but, in addition, an open campaign is being organised for the repeal of the Trade Union Acts. Nearly all tha Chambers of Commerce in the country have passed resolu- tions calling upon the Government to repeal the Trades Dispute Act of 1906, and last week an organisation was launched which had for its object to secure the repeal of that measure. A railway director, who is a member of Parliament, has introduced a Bill into Parliament to repeal the Act, and a considerable number of other members have signified their sympathv with the object. Trade Unionism is in for a big fight. There is no doubt about that. The attacks upon

the Trades Disputes Act of 1906 are direCted mainly against the clause which legalises peaceful picketing; but the demand is made for the repeal of the whole Act. If the Trades Disputes Act were repealed there would be an end of Trade Unionism ; for, without the protection which that measure gives an organisation could not carry on any of the activities for which it exists. That Act does something more than give the legal right to picket by peaceful persuasion. It does three things ; first, it repeals the con- spiracy law ; second it relieves the union funds from liability for the acts of individual members of the union ; third, it legalises peaceful persuasion. THE OLD STATE OF THINGS. Before the passing of the Trades Disputes Act in 1906 the law in regard to trade unions was in a chaotic and conflicting state. In the course of one of the debates on the subject Mr. Asquith, who is a lawyer, said that he " professed his complete inability to reconcile by any form of language he could devise, however vague and elastic, the conflicting and waving dicta of the judges of the land." Before the Trades Disputes Act was passed no trade union knew what it was legally entitled to do. Eminent lawyers confessed that in the then state of the law they were unable to advise trade unions as to their legal powers. Between the Taff Vale decision and the passing of the Trade's Disputes Act the trade unions were in a state of paralysis. It is to this condition that the people who are agitating for the repeal of the Act want to reduce the unions once more. Although the trade unions were ignorant as to what they had a legal right to do after the Taff Vale and some other legal decisions given about the same time, they knew that there were certain things which they had not the legal right to do. If the Trades Disputes Act is repealed the unions will be put back in the old position and the law will stand just as it was at the time the Taff Vale and these other decisions were given.

So it is important for the trade uniOns to fully understand what is involved in this powerful attack which is now b.,ing engineered.

WHAT WILL HAPPEN. If the Trades Disputes Act is repealed three things will be made illegal: (1) The secondary or sympathetic strike. (2) The picketing of works or places of business for the purpose of trying to persuade people to abstain from working. (3) The exemption of trade union funds from liability for the actions of indi- vidual members and agents. Every trade unionist will see at once that these three things go to the very root of trade unionism, and that without the right to declare a sympathetic strike, without the right to picket it would be impossible to successfully conduct a strike. If the Act were repealed, any trade union which passed a resolution to declare a sympathetic strike would be liable to have its funds appropriated as damages. The sympathetic strike is now far more of a weapon than it was at the time when the famous decision in Lyons V. Wilkins was given. This decision was given under the Combination law which makes it illegal for two persons to conspire to do together an act which as individuals and separately they have a full legal right to do. It is very important that trade unionists should bear in mind that if this campaign against the Trades Disputes Act is successful I workmen will be deprived of the right to I come out on strike to assist other workmen I who may be locked out, or on strike. In that case the workmen would be denied a legal right which the Courts declared the Traders I had before the Trades Disputes Act was I passed. It was decided by the Courts in tne Lyons v. Wilkins case that workmen were doing an illegal thing, for which they could be punished, if they came out on strike because they would not supply material to a firm whose workpeople were out; but about the same time, in the Mogul case, the Court I held that " it was not illegal for a trader to I aim at driving a competitor out of trade pro- vided the motive be his own gain by appro- priating the competitor's trade, and the means he uses be legal . . . . and what one trader may do in respect of competition a body or set of traders may do." THE ABOLITION OF PICKETING.

The second thing which would come to pass if the Trades Disputes Act were repealed would be that picketing would be illegal. The law would still hold the primary strike to be legal, but it would declare that the means by which a strike could be made a success were illegal. The present law legalises peaceful persuasion but one would think from the resolutions which are being passed by employers' associations that the Trades Disputes Act has given trades unions power to do just what they think, and has relieved them from all responsibility for their actions. In view of the erroneous impressions whi^h are being given, it is important that it should be generally known what the present law is on the question of picketing. The clause in the Trades Disputes Act which relates to picketing reads as follows : " It shall be lawful for one or more persons, acting on their own behalf or on behalf of a trade union or of an individual employer or firm in contemplation or furtherance of a trade dispute to attend at or near a house or place where a person resides or works or carries on business or happens to be, if they so attend merely for the purpose of peacefully obtaining or communicating information, or of peace- fully persuading any person to work or abstain from working." Nobody who reads some of the more violent denunciations of the Trade's Dispute Act which are now appearing in the press would ever think that the above is all that

the Act does in regard to the right to picket. One paper I have by me says, "The worst section of the Act is, of course, that which legalises peaceful picketing. The clause enables the tyranny of the majority to find the fullest scope. It permits the most thorough-going intimidation, and has led, as it was bound to lead, to riot." The clause Icliies nothing of the sort. It gives no power to trade unions which is not expressly given to an individual employer or to a firm, and it leaves any man who goes an inch beyond the terms of the clause exposed to the penaltits of the ordinary law; and as a matter of fact there have been a number of prosecutions for illegal picketing and intimidation since the Trades Disputes Act was passed.

===== 0002601_19111125_art0063.txt | included=true | included =====
A FUTILE SUGGEgTION.

Those who are agitating for the appeal of the Act say that there has been an increase of violence and intimidation in connection with trades disputes since this Act was passed. There is no evidence to support such a statement. The contrary is more accurate. The most extraordinary thing about the strikes last summer was the general absence of anything like rowdyism; and where anything of the sort did occur it was the action of hooligans who find their opportunty at such a time. It is ridiculous to think that the legal prohibition of picket- ing can prevent violence and intimidation. That would be the very way to increase such und-sirable things. No law would ever put an end to picketing. So long as there are strikes there will be picketing, because no strike can be carried on without it. If the law were to prohibit picketing it would still be done, with the result that all restraint would be taken away from the practice. The repeal of the Trades Disputes Act is just the very best way to encourage such deeds of violence in connection with labour troubles as the employers are now deprecating. The third thing which the repeal of the Act would do would be to make the funds of the Union liable for any illegal action of an individual member of the union. The opponents of the Act talk as if this Act allowed a trade union to do just as it likes. No trade unionist and no trade union official can do any illegal action without being individually responsible for the law of the land. What the opponents want is to make the whole union responsible for the action of one member. . The situation is dangerous. Very powerful . .

tail-den-Ceti-ire-WC worTc to underrnine trade unionism. It is most necessary that the unions should be vigilant.

WHY DO WE SAY PUSS?

Did you ever wond3r why we say "Puss" when we talk to the cat. Cats have their own names as a rule, but we all agree that Pussy is a name that will fit them all. It is one of those words that we use without thought and it is only when we make inquiry that we find how interesting a word it is.

Thousands of years ago in Egypt they were very fond of cats. In fact, they used to worship them. They admired their inscrutable faces and thought that when they sat so still they were seeing visions of the Gods and paradise. They used also to compare the cat with the moon, possibly because they like to go out by moonlight, but chiefly because their eyes change like the moon from crescent to full. They had a God with a cat's face,and they gave it the name of ". !ht" which means

the moons. This word became shortened to pas or puss and travelled over to Europe as a pet name for the cat. I have heard it in France and Belgium and it may be used in many other countries too. How singular to think that a pet name used by the children of Egypt in the days of Moses has been handed down to us Perhaps I ought also to tell you that they . _ . _

thought so much of cats in old Egypt thawt they used to carefully wrap them up and make mummies of them when they died. They buried them in special cemeteries and now sometimes when explorers are digging they find thousands of mummified cats all carefully buried. And what do you think they do with them? They sell the sacred cats to the farmers for manure. Horrible thought

CHAPTER XII.

"THU( I AII—TOUR DAUOHTII." When Boatock shut the door in her face, Helen shrank back in a tumult of conflicting emotions. She was overwhelmed by the sud- denness of the blow which had fallen upon her, by the baffling secrecy with which it had been dealt, and by the dread of the unknown but certainly terrible consequences it threat- ened. .

Her first sensations were those of fierce in- dignation et the wholly unmerited shame thrust upon her, and of passionate rebellion against the monstrous injustice and cruel in- iquity, mingled with an ineffable despair of beinK able to prove the falseness of the

charge. In the expression of horrified surprise sod pain upon Mr. Garwood's face as ha had looked at her just after he had taken the rings from the trunk, she had read the certain verdict of the world upon . . .

the evidence against her. Up till that mo- ment, he had been strong in his faith in her. He was undoubtedly her friend; his pain at every step of the investigation had been un- mistakable; but his faith had been shattered _ .

.t.b.; charge, as she had before had to face the crisis when she had found herself alone and

almost penniless in London. The consciousness of spotless innocence up- held her. She wou:d protest against her guiltlersnesa, and bear without flinching whatever punishment might be wrongfully in. flicted ; and, when it was over, she would at to work to discover the truth and unmask the secret enemy who had dealt this crushing blow. It was no time for panic or for tears., She must fight; and in such a struggle she would need all her strength and courage and wits, if she was to have a hope of winning. In this mood site put on her hat and jacket and went into the two men, declaring that she was ready there and then to face the

charge. Her entrance .discoucerted Bostock con- siderably. His object was to drive her to flight; and he had been endeavouring to co- erce his companion to threaten a prosecution, but to leave Helen time to make her escape. Mr. Garwood had displayed unexpected ob- stinacy, however. He would not prosecute, he declared; and would not even threaten to do so. The matter was in his hands, he said; the rings had been recovered, and so far as he was concerned it was at an end. "There will be no charge to face, Miss Pax. ton," he said at once. Bostock rose. "I can't stay to hear a mat- ter of this sort compromised, Mr. Garwood. It's too serious to be hushed up. My superiors wouldn't hear of it." "Your superiors?" queried Garwood. "You know what I mean," was the pointed reply. "I wash my hands of it; and you must be prepared to take the consequences," and with that he took his hat and left. Who is that man," asked Helen, sharply. "Why does he threaten you with conse- quences? Why does he hound you on to pro- secute me?" Garwood rose to leave. "I would rather not discuss the matter any further. It is . . . .

more painful to me than I Scan describe; but I shalt do no more in it.' _

"I ought to thank you; but I cannot. You believe me guilty?" He turned with a heavy sigh, and gestured without replying. "I understand that. But you are wrong. I declare on my honour that I am as inno- cent as a child unborn. I am the victim of a cunning and cowardly enemy; and some day I shall discover who it is. You cannot believe this; and I do not know that I can blame you. But you have been a friend ; and you shall be the first to whom I bring the knowledge, when I have gained it." He had reached the door and turned. "Why do you think you have an enemy?" be althea. "Can you still be friend enough to listen?" And when he paused, the told him all that had occurred in regard to her loss of work. She told the story well and convin- cingly, and it impressed him. Ile was really anxious to believe in her innocence, and grasped readily at the slender hope her words suggested. "Who was the companion you had here?" be asked after a pause. "She had nothing to do with it," replied Helen, and went on to tell him all about Mary Pescott. Then he questioned her closely ; and every reply appeared to strengthen his conviction that her tale, improbable as it seemed, was nevertheless true. Yet he was sorely per- plexed. "I will help you to the utmost of my power," he said at length; "but another time you must tell me the whole story of your life. If what you state so positively be true—and even in face of what has happened here to-day. I cannot help believing you— then there must be someone who is bent upon ruining you, and that person must be found. I cannot take you back into my office at present, for reasons which I am not free to explain. But I will see you to- morrow, I will come here again; and you shall tell me then. Good night." He held out his hand; but her eyes filled with tears at his unexpected proof of friendship and confidence, and instead of taking his hand she sank into a chair and buried her face in her hands overcome with emotion. "Courage, Miss Paxton, courage," he said, greatly moved by the sight of her tears. "Courage and hope. We shall find out the truth." He stood a second or two in hesita- tion, and then stole out of the room. Helen was inexpressibly touched by his kindness, and sat a long time thinking over all that had passed and how different the result might have been but for that kind- nese; and then, remembering the task which the coming of the two men had interrupted, role to continue it. Bulmer was coming home, and would be again in London in a day or two at most. By that time she must be away from the fiat and hidden in some place where he could not find her; and she had already taken rooms in another part of London. She had been packing up with this object; and had already arranged to sell everything except her clothes, and she forced herself to finish the packing of these before going to bed. The work did her good. It kept her from thinking of her troubles, and tired her so that she slept well; and she rose the next morning refreshed and prepared to begin what she resolved must now be the great object of her life—the discovery of those who were bent upon her ruin, The bargain, with the furniture dealer wl

_..„ soon made, the money was paid, sad th-; goods taken away, when 'he made a dis- covery. The second key of the flat was miss- ing. Mary Pescott mint have taken it away with her, op have lost it. And it must ha 4 been by means of that key that the person had entered who had placed the rings and the safe key in her trunk. She was sitting on ono of the trunks in the empty room when she thought of this: and then she remembered that her friend had certainly had the key when she had seen her last. Was it poasible that Mary Pcsoott could have been in any way, even uncon- 4ciously, a party to what had occurred? If sc., here was a clue to be followed up at once. She must go to her and question her closely Engrossed in the thoughts started by the discovery, oho was surprised by a knock at the door of the Oat, and infinitely more sur- prised when she opened it, to find Sir Gregory Trevellock standing there. In the tumult roused by the occurrences of the previous day she had almost forgotten that she had written to him that they must meet. "I thought it best to come to you," be said, hesitatingly and nervously, as he glanced round in surprise at the empty room into which she led him. "I am going away," she said, in reply to his glance. ",Why?" he asked, not in any trouble?" "Would that distress Toss"

"You are not--ere

' 7-• eV

••I non t think we understand one another, Miss Paxton. I am sorry. I aneure you I wish earnestly, most earnestly, to be your friend." "Are you sure you have been one?" The question came sharply, like an accusation. It was so meant, indeed; and the same memory was in the minds of each—the scene at the Chase.

"You speak very—very curiously," he murmured, at a loss for die word he wished to find. His eyes fell before her steady gate. You do not answer me, and do not look at me, Sir Gregory." "In what way do you think I have not been your friend?" Her reply took a quite unexpected form. Lying on the bare boards at her feet was a sheet of paper. She lit a match and laid it carefully on the paper. "Have you ever tried this experiment F" she said deliberately. He looked up quickly at her and dropped his eyes again on meeting hers, and stared at the match. It burnt itself out, leaving a charred hole. Helen lit another; and in silence they both watched it burn out like the first. This time the paper caught, smouldered a moment, and then went out. As it flickered out, Sir Gregory heaved a deep sigh, and moving back a step or two sat dove on one of the corded trunks, and stared blankly at the floor. "You understand?" said Helen. Ile made no reply. He did understand, and knew that she understood as well, and that she knew what he had done. His lie was found out, and he waited in fear and anguish to learn the use she meant to make of the discovery. "You understand?" die repeated. It was no longer a question. "Why did you send for me?" he asked, when the silence became unbearable. "Why did you destroy the papers I gave you that day? Why did you pretend it was an accident? What harm have I ever done you that you should treat me so?" Then with a start she leapt to the conclusion that all her other troubles were his doing, and her face hardened and fierce anger was in her voice as she added: "And why have you con- tinued to persecute me and seek to put this last foul shame upon me? How could you be so infamous as to seek to ruin me, and have me thrust into gaol?" "I don't understand you," he protested, glancing up in astonishment. "Don't understand?" she repeated scorn- fully, her eyes glowing, and her face aflush with anger. She pointed down to the paper on the floor. "You understand that, at any rate, and so do I. It is the measure of your truthfulness. You are a rich man, titled, and held in honour by toe world. lam a poor girl, helpless, and all but friendless and alone. You lied to me then," and again she pointed to the paper. "and now you have sought to put the shame of this theft upon me, bribing others to lie and deceive and cheat, even as you yourself did then. Why do you do this? That is the question I have sent for you to answer. And you shall answer it, Sir Gregory, aye, and answer for it, too, rich and powerful though you are." "I know nothing of any theft. On my honour—"

'Honour r' she broke in, with stinging con- tempt in her tone, as she pointed down for the third time at the smouldering paper. "Is it worth while to persist in a denial/ It will not avail with me. I see it all now. Your spies were set to discover where I worked, so that I might he ruined with those who employed me, and so rendered penni- less and ready to walk into the trap of the engagement with Mr. Garwood. Then he was entrusted with the jewels in order that your Ties might steal them from him, and plant the robbery on me, and I might be branded as a thief and gent to gaol, ruined, shamed, and disgraced, but out of your path. You are a generous enemy, Sir Gregory; a chivalrous, high-minded, righteous, Christian gentle- man." .

..... He listened without attempting to inter- rupt her. He was shocked and pained by what she said, and by the persecution she had endured; but, while he had shrunk and winced under her vehement denunciation of the wroiig he had really done her, he was un- touched by this false charge. He rose and lifted his hand in protest. "You are doing Inc a rank injustice; I repeat that I know nothing of all that you now say." Her lip curled. "I find that too difficult to believe. In all my life I have had but one enemy—you. Only you, in all the whole wide world of men and women." "I am not your enemy." he protested. "Then why did you destroy the papers I entrusted to you? Ah, you cannot answer that, or, rather, you will not. I think I know, however. As heaven is my witness I dread to think of that reason. You did it because you learnt that my name was not Paxton, but Powell, and that my father's name was the same as that which you once bore—Gregory Powell. I have learnt that since I NM you. Bulmer told Inc. It was then you burnt the proofs of my identity. Why?" He had shrunk back to his seat again, and remained silent, his hands tightly clasped and his eyes once more on the floor, avoiding hers. "From the moment he told me I have not seen him. I shrank from seeing him. I let him go away without meeting him. I dared not, because of the fearsome dread of what your answer would be. You were in Montana at the time my mother married Gregory Powell. That was the name you bore then. He was English, as you are. The likeness I showed you was his, and after t. lapse of years who shall say that it was not one of you? You are still silent. You dare not meet my eyes. Were there two Englishmen of the same name there at that time?" She paused, looking fixedly at him and waiting for the reply, which did not come. He sat pressing his hands now to his head as if in agony. "The man who was coward and scoundrel enough to betray his partner and friend for gold, and then deserted his wife, would be coward enough to destroy what you destroyed when he found that his shame was on the verge of discovery, and to stoop to persecute his child to save his honour. His honour! But he might at least have had courage enough to tell that child the truth. Have you?" With a groan he took his hands from his head and tried to meet her look, but cowered from her and forced himself to answer, speak- ing in slow, broken tones. "You—you are right. There was—but one Gregory Powell." Helen stepped back in horror. "Then I—l am your—your daughter. Oh, God!" She turned away to the window, leaning against it and looking out, but seeing nothing —shinned and dazed by the rush of distract- ing thoughts roused by this confirmation of her worst fear.

(To be continued.)

Stockton Heath. ST. THOMAS' SALE OF WORE.

Considerable interest was taken by the congregation of St. Thomas' Church, Stockton Heath, in the annual sale of work mhich was held in the Hall Cafe on Thursday. The event had been well prepared for by the lady workers, and there was a choice collection of useful and fancy wares. Much taste had been exercised in the arrangement of the stalls which were allocated as follows :—Cburch officers, congregational, Girls' Friendly Society, china, Bible classes, and refreshment. The opening ceremony was performed by Mrs Egerton Warburton, of Arley Hall, in the presence of a large gathering. The Vicar (Rev. James Collins) explained that the principal object of the sale was to assist the curate tund, the income of which had notbcon as large as they would have liked, of recent years. Some of the district visitors kindly collected for it in their respective disticts and raised about Eto a year in that way, which I was very creditable to them. (Hear, hear.) However it took more than .C5O to keep a curate. (Laughter.) They bad bad to advertise for one at £2oo—an increase on what they had previously paid. That was shard parish to work in and they required a curate of ability who would be worth £2OO. They hoped shortie to obtain the services of a good man. Mr. Collins con- cluded by announcing that the late Curate, the Rev. H. A. Lester, bad sent a message wishing the sale every success and stating that he found it impossible to leave his work in London to come amongst them on that occasion. Mrs. Egerton-Warburton said she did not feel • stranger by any means at Stockton Heath, for there were many links of friendship between them. She hoped the sale would be an unqualified success. The object—to provide the stipend of a curate—was one which most appeal to all earnest Church people. They bad been so well provided for in the past that it would not at iirat be apparent to them how great the loan would be if that extra cleriesl help were not forthcom- ing. That was a large perish and the loss would be felt especially by those who were in the need of sympathy— the sick, the friendless and the sorrowful. Money was therefore imperative but It was not easy to get in these days—at least not to spend as they wanted. WOULD-BE SUICIDES ALLEGATION AT RUNCORN.

A remarkable story was told at Runcorn on Monday. when Richard Pugh (25), son of a well-known former, publican, was charged with damaging a rug in the cell, and also with attempted suicide. Superintendent Ennion said the men was wanted on a charge at Wallsend-on- Tyne,and was arrested in Runcorn on Saturday night. At ten o'clock witness went to visit him in his cell, and found him lying on the floor uncon- scious. He bad his bootlace tied tightly round his neck. Witness cut it, and after twenty minutes' artificial respiration prisoner recovered his senses. The man would have died in a few minutes if be had not been found. Prisoner : I Was not going to be knocked about and called a dog. Jam no more a dog than that gentleman —if be calla himself one. It's a lie to say I attempted suicide. All men on public works tie a bootlace to their braces no as to stop their shoulders from being rubbed. Superintendent 'Union asked who had knocked prisoner about. Prisoner : It was too dark to see. They called me, dirty deg. In committing Pugh for twenty-one days, the Chair- man raid he had not helped himself by bringing unfounded charges of that kind.

EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON.

LORD WOLMER AND WOMEN'S SUFFRAGE. Lord Wolmer, M.P., in a speech to the Newton Division Conservative Association on Saturday, referred to mashood suffrage, and said it seemed to him that Mr. Asquith and Mr. Lloyd George wished to find a method of effectually scotching women's suffrage. Mr. Asquith dare not oppose it in the House of Commons, and Mr. Lloyd Georj,e was regarded by the women as a snake in the grass. If manhood suffrage were adopttri It would mean the indefinite postponement of women's suffrage as a quo:ion of practical politics. There were a million more women than men in the country. If every man had a vote, then every woman would have to have one under an adult suffrage bill, mid then them a would hen great majority of women voters. If he knew the men of England, they were not likely to agree to that. That was the reason why Mr. AsyttiLh suddenly pronounced for manhood suffrage. It was a dodge on the part of the Government to sidetrack the legitimate demands of the women who were qualified to haves share in the government of the country.

BREAKING INTO SHOPS. EARLESTOWN MEN SENT FOR TRIAL

At Warrington Occasional Court, on Monday,Michao English, and William Trigg, lodging at 86, Legh-street, Earlestowo, were committed to the Liverpool Quarter Sessions on the charge of breaking and entering the shop of Bradley and Co., Earlestown, and stealing a safe containing £22 in cash and a number of books on Friday, the 10th inst. The evidence was to the effect that an entrance had been effected by removing a , =niter of bricks from the back room window. The safe was removed to the lodgings of English, where it was broken open, and afterwards taken to some waste land near John-street, where it was buried but was recovered by Sergeant Lennie. When arrested, by Sergeant Lennie, Trigg said, in reply to the charge, be had " planted" some of the money. It was no use telling him where, because it would not lighten the sentence. It would came in useful for him when he came out, A further charge of breaking and entering the premises of the Maypole Dairy Company, Legh-etreet, with intent to commit a felony, was preferred against Trigg. On this occasion the safe was removed from a stand into another room. He was committed on this charge also. The Chairman (Mr. C. White) complimented Sergeant Lennie upon the intelligent observation he had displayed.

THE LABOUR POLICY. PARTY ATTACKED BY LORD VFOLMER,

In the course of a speech to a meeting of the Con- servative Divisional Association on Saturday afternoon Lord %V°lmer said the Labour party, since Mr. Ramsay MacDonald became the leader, had, as an independent force, ceased to count. It started by assisting Mr. Asqvith to destroy the power of the House of Lords to refer measures to the people. Then it assisted his. Asquith to stifle debate, and take away practically all the rights of private members, tying the Commons hand and foot in order that they should do implicitly what the Government demanded. The Labour party, which professed to take an interest in industrial questions, had practically made no show in the Insurance Bill debates. Its members had been nothing but the servile servants of Mr. Asquith and Mr. Lloyd George. During the rioting of September from every Labour platform it was declared that Mr. Asquith had better resign at once. They con- demned the Government for sending troops to Man- chester and Liverpool, but had not moved hand or foot to attack in Parliament. It was the same with the Shops Bill and the Coal Mines Bill and the Osborne judgment. The Bill dealing with the Osborne judgment had been dropped, and the Labour party had just pocketed the insult. The only thing they had got was £4OO each a rear. That appeared to keep them sufficiently quiet for Mr. Asquith to do anything he liked with them, and to force them to toe the line to the Liberal party.

Sr. HELENS DOG AND POULTRY SHOW. The sixth annual show run by the employes of the, various department of Messrs. SNOW IN THE HIGHLANDS AND IN WALES. The Scottish Highlands are reported to be in the grip of a very severe snowstorm, the snow lying deeper than it has done at this time for several years past. The higher hills in the Bawdy district of Inverness- shire are covered to a depth of over a foot, and in some localities there have been difficulties and delay In delivering the mails. Much damage has been done to young trees, many saplings having been snapped. Sheep are badly off and have to be hand-fed. Deer have been compelled to seek the low lands and could be seen the other day in large flocks on the sides of the road near the railway line.

BEAUTY IN CRAFTSMANSHIP.

Mr, Henry Ct.:loess, of Manchester, delivered an illustrated lecture on "The Craftsman and his work," under the auspices of the Ogden Trust and the Warrington Museum Committee, in the Masonic Hall, Bold-street, on Saturday evening. Mr. J. Upson presided over a good attendance. Mr. OGDEN said the foundation and superstructure of civilization were built up on craftmanship. On the work of man's band controlled by his mind,and on the adaptation of nature's gifts of materials, bung his whole existence. So the craftsman pursued the noblest calling. Without his constant effort man would degenerate, and mischief would result from his idle hands, whether it be the idleness of the rich Cr the poor, or whether it be compulsory or voluntary, end the savage state would soon return In the early stages simplicity was the key-note in all man's contrivings, not only in the lesser thing, but in the greater also. A Temple or a Cathedral was, in general structure, but little more complex in design than a but or a barn, in fact it had been said that a Cathedral is only a 'glorified barn," although stone was used in one case, and bricks and wood in the other— the chief differences were in magnitude, perma- nency, and elaboration with ornament. We found the constructive featives delightfully emphasized and the beauty of the motives applied so tastefully, that we were lost in admiration, and were charmed with I the pet fections of the many crafts united and co-operating in the production of the harmonious whole under the general supervision of a chief contriver—the architect. Until the middle of last century most crafts bad legally bound apprentices who wore to be tiught the whole of their craft. In return they had but little remuneration and the youth received instruction from a roaster craftsman. In many iustances he lived with his master and before he was admitted to his guild he had to travel to gain fuller experience, so &mg his journeys and becoming a " journeyman." Latterly the subdivision of labour had prevailed and the so called apprentice was run into a grove and he learned only one pwtion of the trade and knew little of the whole ; herein lay the value of technical schools and libraries, and other supplementary aids to study. These gave knowledge in that which the economics of modern production denied. The modern craftsman had many advan- tages in the wonderful discoveries of science. The ideas of expert workers as set forth in innumerable treatises, rapid means of transport, had brought to hand the richest materials from the ends of the earth into the workshop, where the craftsman prepared them for his own special purpos.s. With all these advantages it might be expected that good work would naturally result. This was not, however, always the case—the demand for good work was often limited by the vagaries of fashion and often our very deficiencies were the result of facilities we possess. Formerly people were content with a fear really well made articles, but to-day we contented ourselves with useless things latest fashion—newest elle because we imagined they were woiks of art. Modern life with its many advantages, had also many detractions militating against the best efforts of the workman, such as the dividing of the task into too many sub- divisions, suppression of the name of the worker under the firm's company title, reducing the personal interest to a minimum. taste and feeling were likely to be neglected and thus the completed work was often lifeless. The arts and crafts exhibitions movement was the outcome of an effort to restore the joy of the worker. It was a great revelation to find highl , y educated " outsiders 'exercising their g.ft of research and affecting the whole ideas c,f taste and beauty. Morris Crane and others had led the way and had benefitted not only the handicrafts but also the manu- facturers. Simple and more beautiful furniture, jewellery and other objects weyradually gaining in estimation and though the a bum of illustrated journals and municipal efforts to educate the people, the beauty of old work was not only appreciated, but it was creating a demand which must be for the good of everyone.

ANTI-SOCIALISM.

The Lecturer on Capital and Credit

The second of the series of lectures promoted by the Anti-Socialist Union was held on Tuesday evening in the Masonic Hall, when Mr. C A. Swanton gave a lecture on "Credit.' Mr. H. J. Westbrook presided. At the outset, the secretary, Mr. Chas. McNair, referred to the correspondence which appeared in the local press last Saturday, relative to the complaint of Councillor Locker's, that in the advertisement Mr. McNair had informed the public that tickets and syllabus for the lecture could be obtained at the Liberal Club. Mr. McNair explained that be naturally thought that the Liberal Party as well as the Conservative Party were opposed to Socialism. He did not know whether that was true or not but as a rule it was. Under that impression ha sent a number of tickets to the Liberal Club and as he received no reply be took it for granted they were going to co-operate with them. Why they should take umbrage he could not understand unless there was I some understanding with the Socialists of which they did not know. Whether that was so or not, if the I Secretary objected to distributing the tickets, he might have advised him that he did not intend to do it. Mr. SWANTON pointed out that credit might be I regarded as an extension to capital. Capital in the material rense of the word consisted of land and all other instruments materialistic to production. Credit was an expansion of that by means of which they were able to utilize their intelligence, I character and reputation and convert it into money in order to carry on Borne industry. Whenever credit was given there was the impulse of trust. Reputation aod personality were of the utmost Importance. A bill of exchange and other instruments of credit vastly extended the power of mocey. There were sea to twenty times as many negotiable instru- ments as there was actual money in existence. The enormous power which credit exercised could be abused. The existence of credit wig mainly responsible for those fluctuations known as booms and panics. When there was a general feeling of prosperity there was a tendency on the put of the people to get excited and they pledged their credit to a too great extent and embatked upon transactions beyond their power. As a result of this boom there was an increase of business and industry which led to over production. At the present time there was not such a tendency, people were becoming more level-headed, and realising what lay at the bottom of the theory. Bad times were due to the fluctuations and periods of boom, followed by depression. The method to deal with those periods of depression was to insure against the consequences of them. They were bound to occur, and would continuo to occur. Discuu- ing the effect of credit upon prices, he maintained that if there was an improvement in credit, money increased, because credit and money were practically the same thing. If money increased the prices rose, which meant that goods were produced in greater quantities. There was no reason why they should interfere with it. Things would right themselves if they were left alone. The way to avoid the result of these consequences was Ito look ahead and insure against them. It was an undoubted fact that in recent years there Mid been an increase in the price of necessities. The cost of living had increased. There were various explanations for this rise. One cause was that the world's output of gold had enormously increased, and the commodities had not kept pace with it. They must remember that the price of gold was artificially fixed and if the price of gold could not be dropped the price of commodities must rise to meet it. In conclusion he said that if they would not Imperil their own credit they ought to do nothing which would impair national credit. The CHAIRMAN said he did cot think the members of the Liberal Association ought to have taken umbrage. They ought to have interpreted Mr. hlcNair's overture in the best spirit. Questions having been invited Mr. C. LEE asked what wan meant by the insurance against depression. Mr. SWANTON said he did not want to touch upon a political controversy tut he could see no reason why they should not insure against the consequences of unemployment. If the questioner meant it to refer to traders and business men he thought it was obvious and meant that they should outlive to the full extent of their income when trade was good and find themselves with nothing to fall back upon when trade was had. Don't you think the best cure for unemployment is to end work for the men ? Mr. SNN kNToN Undcubtedly. The only way to deal with It was to accept it as something which they could prevent, and insure the worker against loss of wares. If they altered the currency from gold into paper they would not need as mach credit as at present ? Mr. Sw ANTt.N : I don't think it 'would be safe to alter it. As a matter of fact, there was not sufficient confidence by human beings in each other or nations In each other to allow of the. being done. Ifithey had I that confidence they might do it. Is not that a strong indictment against the present system? Mt. SWANTON : It may be a strong indictment against human nature There was more confidence and credit in Scotland than in Eoglaud. The Cu zi [MI AN I telieve that is due to the fact that there is less socialism. Mr. LEE: No. If the sopply of gold were to become exhausted would it effect the question of credit Mr. STANTON No I Another metal would pro- bably take its place. A vote of thanks to the Lecturer terminated the proceedings.

NEw Ofcranto.—The settlement of Ontario's hinter- land in the northern part of the province is to be vigorously proffecuted,where the existence of 20 million acres of excellent clay laud suitable for clone settle- ment has been established. The Canadian census returns just issued have shown that the rural population of Ontario is at a standstill owing to the drain to the North-west. The possibilities and attractions of Ontario's northland, while differing from those existing in the West, are believed to be just as great; and effective means, therefore, must be taken to bring home to the people the advantages that lie at their doors, and by stopping this leakage to build up Ontario. The ' Monetary Times" thinks that the only practical way to solve the problem of the settlement of Northern Ontario is to appoint a commission of men of ability. energy sod knowledge. It is also suggested that a trial will have to be given to ready-made farms, or at any rate rerni-ready holding. This lean age of rapidity, and it is difficult to induce settlers to take up land from whichthey must clear timber before crops can be grown.

The Shakespeare Society. .Sin—As there appears to be some doubt as to the proposed methods of the Shakespeare Society, I trust you will allow me the oppor- tunity of further explanation. The suggestion is that there shall be monthly meetings arranged in pairs; thp first to he devoted to papers and discussion on A play. the second to readings from the slime play. 'The miner is to be short, not more than -half ran hour, As there is frequently some hesitation in beginning a discussion, it seemed best to provide two openers for the first time; the rest •of the meeting will be open to any members who care to speak on the subject. For the reading, volunteers will be called for at the first meeting; should there be a sufficient number, groups will he formed, who will be invited to get ready different sections of the play. The present Committee will be quite ready to assist in the formation and direction of such groups, or of similar groups for the study of the piny. but as the work will have to he done mainly in the houses of the members, it seems obvious that it must be for the most part a matter of, private arrangement. The Contmittee who are working to organise the Society thought it rield to arrange at least one ordinary meeting. ' As for the choice of the play, though this Society is not • officially connected with. the Shakespearian Dramatic Society. there is no doubt that the hope of its successful formation was aroused by the success of the Macbeth per. formanee. and that, should this hope be ful- filled, it will be largely due to the initiative and energy of Dr. Law, and, in addition, it seemed no small advantage to take a play in which many people were already inte-

rested. _ The choice of a play for other meetings will depend on the wishes of members them- selves. The same may be said of the general aims and methods of the Society. The ulti- mate control of such a Society must rest with its members, and-if there are any whoj are rtally interested in Shakespeare, but are not satisfied with the programme suggested above. I .would invite them to join the Society, mnd then bring forward such im- provements as may seem necessary, and thus bele to make it a success, and to give Me assistance to the anxious to study Shake- speare.—l am, sir, yours faithfully, F. - Evening Schools Criticised. SIR .—I was interested ill reading your reports last Saturday of the prize distribu- tion to students attending the Evening Classes, and noticed that Father Wilson. speaking at Heathside Schools, said—" It was a very common complaint throughout the country that sufficient advantage was not being taken of the facilities and oppor- tunities offered by the continuation schools." and again, " He attributed the comparatively small degree of interest in the Evening Classes vesy largely to the rage for all kinds of amusements." Now, sir, we are bound to admit that amusements do account for a number of yotmg people neglecting their education, but surely this is not the only reason. Is it not a fact that a very large number of intending students are refused admission to the Evening Classes, year after year, because they cannot see their way to take, one of the courses of study, as laid down in the syllabus? Is it not also a fact that students who had been attending the classes for years were refused re-admission to the schools because they would not take one of the

courses? I ask, is it fair that a student should be encouraged to attend the classes, and then when nearing the end of his study be com- pelled to do what the education authorities want him to do or else leave the school? We will suppose that a student had been studying machine construction, stage 2, and was quite agreeable to take a course if that student wanted to continue his machine con- struction where he had left off, he would be put in an advanced course, one of the sub- jects being mathematics. At the end of the year he would be expected to sit at the examinations, rind if he did not pass in all the subjects, he would have to take the course over again. Now, sir, how is a student going to pass the mathematics examination if he has not taken the earlier stages? Yet this is what is expected of him. Then, again, we wilt suppose that a per- son wishes to study bookkeeping. He would have to take the first year commercial course, which includes shorthand. Now it does not matter bow proficient he may be in this subject, he would have to take the elementary shorthand over again, owing to it being included in that particular course. What is wanted is that the course system should be abolished, and then students would be able to centre their minds on one or two subjects instead of having a smattering of many.—Yours faithfully, A REJECTED STUDENT. November 23rd, 1911.

The National Insurance Bill. Sin,—There is a consensus of opinion that national insurance will be a great boon to the nation. There is not, however, the same consensus of opinion that the present Bill is an ideal one by any means. In fact, ninny leading authorities do not hesitate to con- demn it, and to state that in its present form it is an ill-conceived measure, and are urging the Government to call in experts to assist them in making it a sound one. I do not think anyone who is conversant with social and industrial problems can fail to perceive that if the Bill as now framed if passed into law will not only tend to place additional burdens upon the employers and employees, but will also lead to many willing workers being unemployed. It must be borne in mind that, owing to the manner in which the contributory clauses are drawn up, that both the employers and employees will be called upon to pay at least twice, for, in addition to paying their respectir; quotas, they will have to pay their quota as being part of the State. The methods, too, of its administration are also arousing justifiable criticism, for they will not only be costly, but irksome and irritating in the extreme. Many Liberals are becoming deeply con- cerned in the present management of the finances of the State, and their allegiance to the party is being severely strained. They observe that industry, instead of being assisted, is being handicapped, and they wonder what has become of the old policy, " Retrenchment,- which was part of their proud motto, "Peace, Retrenchment, and Reform." No wonder they sigh for the need of a financial genius of the stamp of a Glad- stone. who, instead of placing burdens upon the shoulders of the community, removed them. No one is more conscious than myself that it is the desire of the Government to pass humane measures, but no measure can be deemed humane if. on the one hand, it increases the cost of living, and on the other hand throws men out of the labour market. And that this will take place if the National Insurance Bill is passed into law is certain. for we have the evidence and statements of men like Sir Charles Macara. the leader of the cotton industry, and n Liberal to boot. and unite it host of leading manufacturers as well. One leading manufacturer has gone so far as to state that his firm will part with 300 men, and altogether the outlook of many workers is anything but bright. The fact is that the Government, In their anxiety to pass measures, are acting too hastily in this matter. lam not fit all surprised, therefore. that leading manufac-
